Is Agia a Robot?

Urth list · 163 messages · 2010-06-17 to 2010-06-21

2010urthThe Book of the New SunThe Book of the Long SunTom HopkinsAntónio Pedro MarquesDavid StockhoffTony EllisDavid DuffyJohn WatkinsMark LewinJerry FriedmanJosh GellerMo Holkar / UKGGwern BranwenGerry QuinnJeff WilsonRyan DunnEugene ZaretskiyAdam ThorntonJames WynnRanjit BhatnagarRoy C. Lackey
The thread: Readers debated whether Agia, a central figure in The Book of the New Sun, is a robot, a chem, a paracoita, or a human. Tony Ellis, brunians and Ryan Dunn initially pressed the robot reading using the red fluid, the head-striking sound, and the Long Sun chem passages, while Jerry Friedman, Jeff Wilson and David Stockhoff pushed back with the aureole as backlight and the text's depictions of her eating, drinking and strength. The thread drifted into whether Hethor's lost paracoita is Agia, the genicon and poppet definitions, and the puppet imagery, with Roy Lackey citing chem strength and weight figures to argue she is human. It ended without consensus, with several competing readings left open.
  • Ryan Dunn: Agia is a robot or made being, possibly the missing paracoita, and later Agilus may be the robot
  • David Stockhoff: Agia may be a robot, a paracoita, a human pretender, or an ordinary human, with the thread's evidence unsettled
  • brunians: Agia may be a robot, and she may be Hethor's paracoita who lies about her past
  • Jeff Wilson: the aureole is backlight, Agia is no saint and not a hard robot
  • Roy C. Lackey: Agia cannot be a chem and is human, based on strength, weight and eating evidence
  • Jerry Friedman: argues against the robot reading and points to Thecla and textual features of Agia's appearance

Themes: Robots, androids and artificial life, Ghosts, dreams and visions, Masks, disguise and shapeshifting

2010-06-17 Josh Geller replying to Tony Ellis #

Argues that Agia may be a robot, defending the idea by noting that the red fluid in her body could be hydraulic fluid rather than blood, as in Severian's service. Responds to a Long Sun quotation about chems by pointing out that Severian's attention to detail during an execution may explain why he would not notice the colour difference.
supports · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is a robot

I did not remember that reference.

Thanks.

It does not nullify my hypothesis.

We used to call hydraulic fluid red juice, or cherry juice in the service.

That is because it is red, very much in the way blood is red.

There is a very slight difference in color.

I don't know that you'd notice, if you were focussed on bringing off as difficult an act as a public execution with the attention to detail that Severian brings to everything he does.

.

.

2010-06-17 Tony Ellis #

Argues that Agia was human at first but became a robot, pinpointing the change to Severian's sighting of her in a torn pavonine gown in chapter XVI. Supports the case by pointing to Dorcas's bell-like voice in chapter XXVII and jokingly extends it to Dorcas as Severian's grandmother and Severian himself as a robot.
proposes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia was human before becoming a robot; Dorcas is a robot; Severian is a robot

Agia is definitely a robot, and of a particularly expensive kind. But she was initially human. We can actually pinpoint the moment her robofication begins, when Severian sees her outside the rag shop:

She wore a pavonine brocade gown of amazing richness and raggedness, and as I watched her, the sun touched a rent just below her waist, turning the skin there to palest gold.
TSOTT, chapter XVI

Dorcas is also a robot:

'He wears armor,' Dorcas called again. 'This man has not even a shirt.' Her voice, always so soft before, rang in the twilight like a bell.
TSOTT, chapter XXVII

Bells are metal, just like hammers. How much more of a clue do you need? Of course, she's also Severian's grandmother so it follows that he is a robot too.

That must be why all those cogs and wires fell out when Severian cut Agilus's head off.

2010-06-17 Jerry Friedman replying to brunians #

Disputes the robot hypothesis about Agia by citing Eftls passages on Maytera Marble's mechanical arm, arguing that Agilus's neck and blood in the beheading scene would have differed had he been a chem. Responds to a post's claim that Severian's headsman experience and boot-cleaning show nothing unusual, and rebuts the sexbot idea with the sound of the head striking stone.
disputes · close-textual-reading · source-and-allusion · theories: Agia is a robot or chem; Agilus was a chem built like Marble

From: "[email]" <[email]>
[quotations on chems from BotLS]

nullify my hypothesis.

Here are two more:

"'It's not just reuniting the metal, Patera. There are little tubes in there, tiny tubes, and wires, and things like threads--fibers, they're called--that pipe light.'"

EftLS, Ch. 2

"... he took off the white towel that had covered her [Maytera Marble's] now-lifeless right hand and held it up, in appearance the hand of an elderly woman. A short cylinder of silvery metal extended from its wrist. 'I lost some fluid,' she told him, 'but not very much. There are valves and things to control that...."

[Marble holds her stump up. Swallow tells her] "'Pretend that you're going to grab hold of my nose.'

"Minute glimmerings appeared in the shadowy interior of the stump of arm, pinpoint gleams...."

EftLS, Ch. 7

I think these make your hypothesis very hard to sustain. If Agilus were a chem built like Marble, his neck would probably have been sheathed in metal and would certainly have contained wires, tubes, and optical fibers, not flesh and bone. This would have done /Terminus Est/ no good and would surely have felt and looked different from cutting through a human neck.

Maybe there was another kind of robot. But I doubt very much someone would make robots with necks that acted just like human necks when cut, but with skulls that sounded different when banged into stone walls.

much in the way blood is red.
in color.

The fluid that spurts out of a Vironese soldier when Mint severs its leg with an azoth is black, by the way (CotLS, Ch. 9).

attention to detail that

Severian makes a general remark in that scene about how a well-done beheading feels to the headsman. I take that to mean he felt nothing different about Agilus's head. And see below.

From: David Stockhoff <[email]>
[...]

He also cleans his boots. By that time the blood would have at least started to clot, and he'd have leisure to notice its texture and smell, since he'd no longer be focused on bringing off the execution. He must have cleaned /Terminus Est/, too.

Also, the blood frightened the baggage sumpter (presumably a horse), which would have been by smell, not color.

Agilus was the heir of an optimate and impoverished himself by having a sexbot made to look like a female version of him or like his real sister.

No, I don't believe it. And I repeat that Severian doesn't say Agia's head hitting the stone wall sounded like a hammer hitting stone. He said it sounded as /sharp/, not as metallic or ringing.

It's interesting, though, how things can be read differently when I have this theory in mind. "Agia shrugged, and the moonlight turned her bare shoulders into something more precious and more beautiful than flesh." (/Claw/, C. VIII) There are a couple other examples like that.

Jerry Friedman

2010-06-17 Tony Ellis replying to Tony Ellis #

Quotes Long Sun passages that describe chems as metal and synthetic constructions made by bios, including Maytera Marble's metal fingers and Incus's severed primary cable. Answers Tony Ellis's question about whether Wolfe's word 'chems' means mechanical, offering the quoted passages as evidence without further comment.
informs · close-textual-reading · theories: Chems are metal and synthetic constructions

"Chems are wonderful constructions, a race that we bios created long ago, our own image in metal and synthetics."
LOTLS, chapter seven.

"No, no." Maytera Marble's one functioning hand gripped the arm of her chair so tightly that the upholstery rose like dough between its metal fingers.
EFTLL, chapter five

Auk was peering at the mass of mechanisms the plate had concealed. "I see little specks of light."
Incus was triumphant. "...His primary cable has been severed, and those are the ends of the fibres."
COTLS, chapter two

Whole message, with quotes · original

>Gene calls robots 'chems'.

>Does he call them that because they are mechanical?


"Chems are wonderful constructions, a race that we bios created long
ago, our own image in metal and synthetics."
LOTLS, chapter seven.

"No, no." Maytera Marble's one functioning hand gripped the arm of her
chair so tightly that the upholstery rose like dough between its metal
fingers.
EFTLL, chapter five

Auk was peering at the mass of mechanisms the plate had concealed. "I
see little specks of light."
Incus was triumphant. "...His primary cable has been severed, and
those are the ends of the fibres."
COTLS, chapter two

2010-06-18 Ryan Dunn replying to Jerry Friedman #

Proposes that Agia may be a robot clone and Agilus a cacogen or hierodule, and asks about the runes and map-like design Agia scratches into the ground. Cites Severian's passages on the scratched design and the aureole of light around Agia and Agilus's joined heads, and quotes the chem evidence from EftLS and BotLS that the poster's earlier hypothesis was tested against.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is a robot clone; Agilus is a cacogen or hierodule

Or what about:

Agilus as cacogen (hierodule?), and Agia as robot clone.

Also, have you guys already discussed what were the runes and strange maplike design she scrawled into the ground where she had thrown down Severian's orichalk?

"When I left him, the orichalk was gone. In its place - and no doubt with its edge - a design had been scratched on the filthy stones. It might have been the snarling face of Jurupari, or perhaps a map, and it was wreathed with letters I did not know."

Or the aureole of light which surrounded Agia and Agilus's joined heads when Severian barged in on them laying naked together face-to-face?

"Inside a naked man lay upon straw. A chain ran from the iron collar bout his neck to the wall. A woman, naked too, bent over him, her long, brown hair falling past her face and his so that it seemed to unite them."

"He stared at his hands, slender and rather soft, where they lay in the narrow beam of sunlight that had given his head, and Agia's, an aureole a few moments before."

It's all probably trivial for some, but I find these two characters unsettlingly important to the series for some reason, and wish I could better understand their weirdness.

...ryan

2010-06-18 Jeff Wilson replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that the aureole around Agia and Agilus's joined heads in The Claw of the Conciliator is just backlit hair scattering sunlight, not a supernatural sign, and rejects the robot-clone and cult readings. Responds to Ryan Dunn's proposal that Agia is a robot clone by offering a photographic explanation and linking to an image of a backlit cat.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is a robot clone; Agia is part of a mystery cult

Dude, top posting and not-trimming quoted material makes the baby Theoanthropos cry.

This sounds very much like some folk magic, suggesting Agia is part of a mystery cult.

The aureaole is the scattering of the bright sunlight by someone's backlit hair, giving a halo effect. Here is a similar effect with a kitty:

http://media.photobucket.com/image/backlit%20afro/caitlinjean/backlit-1.jpg

http://preview.tinyurl.com/29jlag7

2010-06-18 Josh Geller replying to Mark Lewin #

Argues that Agia and Agilus, if they are robots, differ from Jonas, who received human spare parts, because they would have been built to be as they are. Responds to Mark Lewin's question about whether the characters are chemical, noting that several posts have shown some of them contain mechanical parts.
supports · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia and Agilus are robots

That's what I was thinking.

Since then there have been several posts showing that at least some of them have some mechanical parts.

I don't think that Agia and Agilus, if they are robots, are the same as Jonas, who was given human spare parts. They would have been crafted to be as they are.

The theory, which I still like, is indeed getting a bit convoluted.

I will certainly talk about it with Gene if and when I get another chance to do so.

Jerry Friedman

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Jerry Friedman #

Disputes the claim that Agia is a robot, arguing that Severian's word "sharp" for the sound of Agia's head striking the wall describes an ordinary skull impact, not metal. Responds to Jerry Friedman, calling Long Sun robot evidence circumstantial and citing Claw's moonlit shoulders simile and the books' shapeshifting.
disputes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is a robot

Well, naturally he doesn't say it sounds "metallic." And you're right that it doesn't ring. But what else could "sharp" mean? Why not then "as sharp as the tap of a carpenter's mallet on a block of oak"? Why not "the thud of an ax stroke in the forest"?

Skulls are rigid bags of blood and brain. I haven't done any testing, but they can't sound anywhere near as sharp as a mason's hammer hitting stone. Not when hitting anything. Ever. The attack and decay are going to be completely different. Unless perhaps the bone breaks, and then there will be a wet crack. Is Agia then an alien who can break her skull and heal in seconds?

What other function could the description serve? It could mean Agia really got a knock---and indeed she buckled. But then she wept---she didn't pass out or gush brains.

Also, the evidence from Long Sun is important but has to be treated as circumstantial: (1) only Severian's descriptions of robots really count, otherwise it's apples vs oranges, because his worldview is profoundly different from Silk/Horn's (2) the technology is not necessarily the same and in some cases may be objectively different.

Again, both books can be seen as long exercises in deliberately obscured character shapeshifting. We have the inhumi, dreamwalking, eidolons, Jonas, Jolenta, Maytera Marble, Baldanders, passing from life to death and back again, and on and on. For a passage like this to signify nothing would be a grave mistake.

No, I don't believe it. And I repeat that Severian doesn't say Agia's head hitting the stone wall sounded like a hammer hitting stone. He said it sounded as /sharp/, not as metallic or ringing.

It's interesting, though, how things can be read differently when I have this theory in mind. "Agia shrugged, and the moonlight turned her bare shoulders into something more precious and more beautiful than flesh." (/Claw/, C. VIII) There are a couple other examples like that.

Jerry Friedman

Disputes Jeff Wilson's suggestion that the aureole around Agia's head implies she is a robot, arguing that the halo is a natural backlight effect. Mocks the reading by mimicking Severian's narration with pointless remarks about rain, sunlight and air, and cites a backlit photo of a cat to show the effect.

Perhaps "At that moment I realized that Agia and Agilus were holy in their unity, because they wore a halo like that of the saints in my little book. They were like gods. I kneeled to them and prayed and said a few Hail Maries for good measure"?

"Chapter XVII: The sky seemed to be releasing a liquid very much like water. Was it in fact rain?"

I could go on.

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to brunians #

Argues that Wolfe's references to robots, chems and the Agia-Agilus pair in The Book of the New Sun and The Book of the Long Sun are deliberate, treating robots as blasphemous creations of an inferior maker. Responds to Brunians' claim about hydraulic tubing by reading Wolfe's coined term 'chems' as a radical choice, and cites Hethor digging bots and the Tolkien comparison of Sauron.
proposes · source-and-allusion · theology-and-religion · theories: Agia and Agilus are a union of bot and human; Robots are blasphemous work of an inferior Creator; Wolfe coined chems to avoid robot cliche

I'd read this more as Wolfe musing, "How will I call robots without sounding like ad copy for a Japanese toy? Hm, well the inventor of robots called them chemical in nature. This runs against the cliche. Thus, applying Brunians' radical principle, I shall call them "chems." "

He faced this problem with Long Sun, where robotness was known and accepted as part of what is not hidden (the local world created by the local archon).

With New Sun, he did not face this problem, because he said instead, "I will not refer to robots at all; they are hidden." On Urth, they are part of the Outside, not the Inside.

And yet both universes are full of robots, half-robots, future robots, former robots, humans on their way to becoming robots/robots becoming humans. Perhaps the halo around Agia and Agilus is a blessing of a union between bot and human as much as man and woman.

Furthermore, I read robots as being almost blasphemous in their unnaturalness, plainly the work of an inferior Creator, although not at all evil. It's a small step from there to sexbots, which are already available in Japan.

(In fact, I can't think of anything more perfectly blasphemous than a pair of sexy male/female twins mating and creating a race of superbots. Dr Who would have stopped them in the nick of time; Severian would kill them. Perhaps Severian's story is in part a record of his stopping various potential future histories so that only one remains.)

Recall that Tolkien made a big deal of explaining that Sauron and his predecessor could not create life, only twist it.

Brunians, where is the line telling us that Hethor digs bots?

2010-06-18 Mark Lewin replying to brunians #

Points readers to Ted Chiang's short story 'Exhalation' as a comparison for the idea that Agia might be a robot, noting its all-metal world and argon-breathing inhabitants. Offers no argument of its own, only a link and a quoted blurb describing the story.
informs

I can't help but think of Ted Chiang's excellent short story 'Exhalation' in relation to this, which is available online here:

http://www.nightshadebooks.com/Downloads/Exhalation%20-%20Ted%20Chiang.pdf.

" 'Exhalation' is an evocative story of an all-metal world, its argon-breathing inhabitants, and a scientist who performs the ultimate self-examination. "

Mark

It's one of Hethor's rants, where he goes on and on about his paracoita.

> I'd read this more as Wolfe musing, "How will I call robots without
> sounding like ad copy for a Japanese toy? Hm, well the inventor of
> robots called them chemical in nature. This runs against the cliche.
> Thus, applying Brunians' radical principle, I shall call them "chems." "
>
> He faced this problem with Long Sun, where robotness was known and
> accepted as part of what is not hidden (the local world created by the
> local archon).
>
> With New Sun, he did not face this problem, because he said instead, "I
> will not refer to robots at all; they are hidden." On Urth, they are
> part of the Outside, not the Inside.
>
> And yet both universes are full of robots, half-robots, future robots,
> former robots, humans on their way to becoming robots/robots becoming
> humans. Perhaps the halo around Agia and Agilus is a blessing of a union
> between bot and human as much as man and woman.
>
> Furthermore, I read robots as being almost blasphemous in their
> unnaturalness, plainly the work of an inferior Creator, although not at
> all evil. It's a small step from there to sexbots, which are already
> available in Japan.
>
> (In fact, I can't think of anything more perfectly blasphemous than a
> pair of sexy male/female twins mating and creating a race of superbots.
> Dr Who would have stopped them in the nick of time; Severian would kill
> them. Perhaps Severian's story is in part a record of his stopping
> various potential future histories so that only one remains.)
>
> Recall that Tolkien made a big deal of explaining that Sauron and his
> predecessor could not create life, only twist it.
>
> Brunians, where is the line telling us that Hethor digs bots?
>

2010-06-18 Ryan Dunn replying to brunians #

Asks whether Agia is a robot, noting her tinny head snapping against a wall beneath her hair, and treats her intended pairing with Hethor as already settled. Responds to brunians' proposal that Agia was Hethor's paracoita and that Agilus was a paracoitus, using the text's own description of her head as the evidence.
questions · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is a robot

Well nobody is questioning the fact that Agia was to be with Hethor, right? That is pretty well stated in the text.

We're now trying to find out what the eff is up with her tinny head snapping against the wall beneath those abundant locks of hair.

...ryan

2010-06-18 Ryan Dunn replying to brunians #

Argues that the genicon poppet in Hethor's rant in The Shadow of the Torturer is Agia, and that Agia is therefore a robot or made being. Supports the claim by quoting Hethor's rant and three passages where Agia, Agilus and Severian discuss her, including the old sailor and the lemon-wood box.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is Hethor's genicon poppet; Agia is a robot

"M-m-master, when I was on the Quasar I had a paracoita, a doll, you see, a genicon, so beautiful with her great pupils as dark as wells, her i- irises purple like asters or pansies blooming in summer, Master, whole beds of them, I thought, had b-been gathered to make those eyes, that flesh that always felt sun-warmed. Wh-wh-where is she now, my own scopolagna, my poppet? Let h-h-hooks be buried in the hands that took her! Crush them, Master, beneath stones. Where has she gone from the lemon-wood box I made for her, where she never slept at all, for she lay with me all night, not in the box, the lemon-wood box where she waited all day, watch-and-watch, Master, smiling when I laid her in so she might smile when I drew her out. How soft her hands were, her little hands. Like d-d-doves. She might have flown with them about the cabin had she not chosen instead to lie with me."

(Hethor's rant in SotT, Chap. 30, Night)

.

And it is verified this poppet may be Agia, in the following passages....

.

"Do you see rings or earrings? A silver lamia twined about my neck? Are my arms constricted with circlets of gold? If not, you may safely assume I have no officer of the Household Troops for my paramour. There's an old sailor, ugly and poor, who presses me to live with him."

(Agia to Severian in SotT, Chap. 19, Botanic Gardens)

.

"Everything you suspect is true - is that enough? I will be dead tomorrow, and shewill wed the old man who dotes on her, or someone else. I wanted her to do it sooner."

(Agilus on Agia in SotT, Chap. XX, Agilus)

.

"...you were trying to kill me before Saltus."

"With the avern. Yes, of course."

"And afterward. Agia, I know who Hethor is."

I waited for her to reply, but she said nothing.

"On the day we met, you told me there was an old sailor who wanted you to live with him. Old and ugly and poor, you called him, and I could not understand why you, a lovely young woman, should even consider his offer when you were not actually starving. You had your twin to protect you, and a little money coming in from the shop."

It was my turn to be surprised. She said, "I should have gone to him and mastered him. I have mastered him now."

(Severian and Agia in SotL, Chap. 15, He Is Ahead of You!)

.

...ryan

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Jerry Friedman #

Disputes the claim that Hethor's lost paracoita from Shadow Chapter XXX is Agia, arguing the description of a doll or genicon does not sound like her. Quotes Hethor's speech and a dictionary definition of genicon, then suggests the robots' power source is probably neither nuclear nor solar, since they rest in tunnels.
disputes · close-textual-reading · source-and-allusion · theories: Hethor's paracoita is not Agia; Agia's power source is not nuclear or solar

"M-m-master, when I was on the Quasar I had a paracoita, a doll, you see, a genicon, so beautiful with her great pupils as dark as wells, her i-irises purple like asters or pansies blooming in summer, Master, whole beds of them, I thought, had b-been gathered to make those eyes, that flesh that always felt sun-warmed. Wh-wh-where is she now, my own scopolagna, my poppet? Let h-h-hooks be buried in the hands that took her! Crush them, Master, beneath stones. Where has she gone from the lemon-wood box I made for her, where she never slept at all, for she lay with me all night, not in the box, the lemon-wood box where she waited all day, watch-and-watch, Master, smiling when I laid her in so she might smile when I drew her out. How soft her hands were, her little hands. Like d-d-doves. She might have flown with them about the cabin had she not chosen instead to lie with me. . . . "

genicon
(Adult / Slang)
My only source for this word is from K <http://www.definition-of.com/K>. S. Daly's * Sex. An Encyclopedia for the Bewildered * (1995): ' A sexual partner <http://www.definition-of.com/partner> imagined by a woman <http://www.definition-of.com/woman> who is no longer satisfied with her real partner <http://www.definition-of.com/partner>.'
(Noun) also as a succubus-real imagined lover. an hallucinated sexual partner

/Scopolagna/: ?A woman whose appearance others find stimulating in the extreme? (GW).

Doll or robot, this does not sound like Agia to me. But it's impossible to say.

As for energy sources, that's an important point. They probably are not nuclear (as we know it) or solar (they rest in tunnels).

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that Agia cannot be the doll Hethor mourns, and that Agia's control over Hethor and the doll's history raise a question about who stole and woke the doll. Grounds this in Hethor's quoted lament for his paracoita, the 'Conservation of Characters' principle, and the idea that Agia must have been under another's control on the Quasar.
disputes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is not Hethor's paracoita doll; Agilus stole and woke the doll

This doesn't make Agia Hethor's doll. He is missing his doll and would no doubt accept a replacement, not caring whether it is flesh or synthetic, but probably preferring synthetic.

But given another Wolfean principle, Conservation of Characters, it makes sense. Agia knows about Hethor's powers and does what she needs to do to control him. However, if she laid in his box before she mastered him ... then she was not under her own control on the Quasar.

So we have a sex doll who was pliant, was apparently stolen, then found, but she now seeks and gains control of her owner. Who stole her? Who "woke" her? Agilus?

This is a whole space opera subplot in itself.

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Suggests that Agia could be a paracoita, a robot-like being, and that she may have changed her eye colour to purple to please Hethor and later changed it again to evade him. Responds to Ryan Dunn's point that Agia's eyes were never purple and that she admits being forced into submission by Hethor's monsters.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is a paracoita; Agia changed her eye colour to evade Hethor

Ah, that explains what I wondered about a minute ago: Hethor controlled her with monsters.

Anyway, while I don't know why Agia would change her eye color, I don't see why she couldn't ... IF she is a paracoita.

Possibly she changed her color to purple for Hethor, then changed it to evade him. And doesn't go back because it is after all an unnatural color.

Points to Agia's own claim in the text that she mastered him, and to the fact that Hethor's toy has gone missing, while cheerfully endorsing a space-opera subplot.

2010-06-18 Ryan Dunn replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Agia may be an artificial being brought from the Quasar, citing her strength and the lemonwood box, and asks whether she ever sleeps. Grounds the case in Agia's conversation with Severian about Hethor and her knife attack in The Sword of the Lictor, reading her blow's strength as betraying her nature.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is a robot brought from the Quasar

More evidence to ponder, and arguments to make for both sides of the argument of whather Agia was brought from the Quasar where she waited in a Lemonwood box without sleeping. Does she ever sleep in Severian's recount?

.

"I thought you had only promised yourself to him, if he would kill me."

"I have promised him that and many other things, and so mastered him. He is ahead of you, Severian, waiting word from me."

"With more of his beasts? Thank you for the warning. That was it, wasn't it? He had threatened you and Agilus with the pets he had brought from other spheres."

She nodded. "He came to sell his clothes, and they were the kind worn on the old ships that sailed beyond the world's rim long ago, and they weren't costumes or forgeries or even tomb-tender old garments that had lain for centuries in the dark, but clothes not far from new. He said his ships?all those ships?became lost in the blackness between the suns, where the years do not turn. Lost so that even Time cannot find them."

"I know," I said. "Jonas told me."

"After I learned that you would kill Agilus, I went to him. He is iron- strong in some ways, weak in many others. If I had withheld my body I could have done nothing with him, but I did all the queer things he wished me to, and made him believe I love him. Now he will do anything I ask. He followed you for me after you killed Agilus; with his silver I hired the men you killed at the old mine, and the creatures he commands will kill you for me yet, if I don't do it here myself."

(Agia and Severian on Hethor in SotL, Chap. 15, He Is Ahead of You!)

.

But she is abnormally strong, as Severan observes here...

.

"Women and tailors hold the blade beneath the hand, according to the proverb, but Agia stabbed up to open the tripes and catch the heart from below, like an accomplished assassin. I turned only just in time to block her blade with the shutter, and the point drove through the wood to show a glint of steel.

The very strength of her blow betrayed her."

(Severian on Agia in SotL, Chap. 15, He Is Ahead of You!)

.

The strength betrayed what? That she was not a woman? The paragraph opens explaining how "women" hold a blade. At first you assume Agia's assassin hold just makes her not a proverbial "woman". But then when he says her strength betrayed here, you're all like HUH?

...ryan

.

.

I always pictured her as Asian. Consistent with the 80's sex doll subculture, I guess.

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that Agia and Hethor's conflicting accounts of their relationship can be reconciled, so that a pliant slave and a mastering woman are the same pair at different moments, and asks what changed. Grounds this in Agia's own words about having mastered him and in the idea that characters speak from divergent perspectives, here framed as a clash between science fiction and picaresque genres.
proposes · close-textual-reading · genre-and-craft · theories: Agia and Hethor are the same pair at different times; Agia's story reflects a picaresque genre perspective

I think now it makes perfect sense. Hethor describes a pliant slave. She says she mastered him---previously, she had not mastered him---she was not the master. She could have been nothing, per her story to Severian, but she says she "should have mastered him." There was a "before" before Hethor pursued her as a suitor.

(She could even be changing "Hethor chased me with monsters whom I slew" to "I am just a poor girl with only a dirty old sailor as a suitor. He wishes to marry me but I spoke a word and now he is my puppy." Wolfe characters have divergent perspectives that direct their speech in reference to identical events. In this case, it's a clash of genres: sci-fi vs picaresque.)

Therefore they are the same. What changed?

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that Agia need not be lying about her circumstances, suggesting Hethor may have used a pretext to pursue her or she opened the shop to catch him. Builds on the quoted passage about her promise to Hethor and questions whether she is male or a robot, offering Wolfe-style hints that she is 'not a woman.'
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is a robot; Agia is a man

Below...

Agia is not necessarily lying. Hethor may have used a pretext to pursue her; she may have opened the shop to catch him, since sailors probably frequent pawns hops and such.

In Wolfe-speak, this means "Agia is a man." Or at least, "Agia is not a woman."

Which? Agilus or sexbot?

If I had withheld my body I could have done nothing with him, but I did all the queer things he wished me to, and made him believe I love him. Now he will do anything I ask. He followed you for me after you killed Agilus; with his silver I hired the men you killed at the old mine, and the creatures he commands will kill you for me yet, if I don't do it here myself."
>
> (Agia and Severian on Hethor in SotL, Chap. 15, He Is Ahead of You!)
>
>   
Agia is not necessarily lying. Hethor may have used a pretext to pursue 
her; she may have opened the shop to catch him, since sailors probably 
frequent pawns hops and such.

> .
>
> But she is abnormally strong, as Severan observes here...
>
> .
>
> "Women and tailors hold the blade beneath the hand, according to the proverb, but Agia stabbed up to open the tripes and catch the heart from below, like an accomplished assassin. I turned only just in time to block her blade with the shutter, and the point drove through the wood to show a glint of steel.
>
> The very strength of her blow betrayed her."
>
> (Severian on Agia in SotL, Chap. 15, He Is Ahead of You!)
>
> .
>
> The strength betrayed what? That she was not a woman? The paragraph opens explaining how "women" hold a blade. At first you assume Agia's assassin hold just makes her not a proverbial "woman". But then when he says her strength betrayed here, you're all like HUH?
>
> ...ryan
>
>   
In Wolfe-speak, this means "Agia is a man." Or at least, "Agia is not a 
woman."

Which? Agilus or sexbot?

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Proposes that Agia in The Shadow of the Torturer is an artificial sex doll who can alter her appearance and sex, perhaps making herself resemble Agilus to please him. Grounds this in the quoted descriptions of her resemblance to her brother and in Severian's statement that she found a protector, answering Ryan Dunn's remark about her Asian appearance and a recent post about the cost of a sexbot.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is a sex doll who changes her appearance; Agia made herself resemble Agilus to please him

It would be typical of Wolfe to hint that two characters are the same, then stamp NOT THE SAME on them, and then make the explanation for why they are the same anyway be the essence and purpose of the character.

Riddle: "I am a hottie who can change her eye color. What am I?"

I'd guess she can change her sex, too. Why build a sexbot that is only one gender? How inefficient. Could she not then also make herself resemble someone? A recent post commented that Agilus would have been bankrupted by purchasing a sexbot that looked like him. But that's not the only possible explanation.

Perhaps the sex doll found a protector in Agilus, as Severian actually states, then made herself look like Agilus because that's what he's into.

Do we have a theory?

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Mo Holkar / UKG #

Argues that Agia can be a robot and still call herself Agilus's sister, because a robot built to match him would naturally speak of their shared flesh. Responds to Mo Holkar's objection that the phrase "the same flesh" would be strange for a robot, and points to the Jahlee episode as a parallel.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is a robot made to resemble Agilus

But it would be not at all strange for a robot who had to believe she was Agilus' sister in order to be Agilus' sister. If she made herself like him, curve for curve, color for color, then that is a precise and poetic way of stating it---especially from her perspective.

See "Jahlee."

2010-06-18 Ryan Dunn replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that the blow Agia strikes in the passage shows she is more than a woman and more than an assassin, which is why the narrator finally accepts her as something other than human. Grounds this in the narrator's earlier knowledge of her three attempts on his life, and reads António Pedro Marques's 'betrayed' as the cue for this reading.
proposes · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is more than woman or assassin

Hmm...

I can't help but think that he was explaining the difference between Agia and other women in this passage. And that the strength of her blow sort of sealed the deal for him, and betrayed that she is something more than woman. And not just assassin, I don't think. He already knew that fairly well, with more than three attempts on his life on her hands (oh those soft, delicate hands!).

...ryan

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that the proverb about women does not show Agia is a robot or a sex doll, since it may refer to any woman, and questions how a doll-like companion became a skilled murderer and who controls whom. Responds to Ryan Dunn's reading of Agia's strong blow as proof she is more than a woman, and draws a comparison with the identity deception in An Evil Guest.
disputes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is a robot; Agia is a sex doll turned assassin

Well, the proverb doesn't describe female assassins---just women. So it's ambiguous. She could certainly be a trained or experienced female assassin, in addition to being either nonhuman or nonfemale. But since Wolfe probably made up the "proverb," it was with a purpose, and there are no clues that Agia is simply an angry trained human female assassin. (Unless she wears secret assassin armor on her head.)

The theory requires either that a sex doll be strong or that a strong robot can be a sex doll.

Either that a sex doll is capable of close combat or that a fighting robot can be a sex doll.

Either that a sex doll that can be whatever her partner wants her to be can be an assassin, or that an assassin can be whatever her target needs her to be.

Is Agia an assassin robot straight out of Dune who played sex doll for Hethor because she wanted his power and money but feared his monsters, or is she a sex doll who turned skillfully murderous? Either way, what changed? How did she get control of Hethor? or did she?

As an aside, it's remarkable how this discussion has begun to resemble that for AEG, where characters almost openly adopt disguises and deal in identity deception, and where a play is actually part of the plot. I always assumed New Sun characters were more stable than they appear to me now. But then I never figured out the Silk vs Horn thing either.

On Jun 18, 2010, at 1:51 PM, David Stockhoff wrote:

> Is Agia an assassin robot straight out of Dune who played sex doll for Hethor because she wanted his power and money but feared his monsters, or is she a sex doll who turned skillfully murderous?

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that Agia may be a robot and speculates whether her love for Agilus is real and holy, possibly a leak of the Outsider, and how that would affect time-travel and age differences between the twins. Grounds this in the halo scene, the gnostic twin-god-rulers scheme, and the possibility that she can reverse direction and rejoin Agilus at any age.
proposes · theology-and-religion · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: Agia is a robot; Agia and Agilus love is holy

Well, that sounds like a very feminine revenge plot, or like a woman who actually feels something for Hethor. I don't think so. I see Agia as having been homicidal and power-hungry for a long time, regardless of her physical makeup.

Here's a thought---would Agia-as-robot have any such human feelings? Does she feel love for Agilus? The halo scene suggests so. I don't know whether the gnostic twincestuous god-rulers scheme requires love or even allows it, or if it matters. (It does in the pieces of gnosticism that survived as alchemy.) I ask because it's possible that the love between A/A is real and that it is actually holy in the God-is-love sense---a bit of the Outsider leaking in.

Another thought: did Agia land, escape, and find Agilus? Or is Urth and Agilus her home base? Would it be possible, given what we know of time travel in the Urth universe, for a twin pair to stay close in age if they want to, when one is a star sailor and would stay young? Probably, since she can reverse direction and return to him.

If Agia is a robot, that problem goes away, as she can always look like Agilus at any age. But she can still sign onto ships that take her back to when Agilus is alive---she could be physically younger than Agilus, or could spend decades with Agilus in the process of living hundreds of years to his thirty or so.

Stop me if I'm starting to sound as crazy as the rest of you.

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Agrees with Ryan Dunn that the detailed description of the missing poppet invites a mystery reading, and argues that her behaviour and appearance differ from Agia's, so the case can be explained without breaking Urth's rules. Proposes that a ruthless ship's robot wearing a woman's skin could be the missing woman, while admitting this stretches the text.
supports · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is a ruthless ship's robot in a woman's skin; The missing poppet is not Agia

I agree. I was just saying the behavior and appearance of the poppet are completely different from the behavior and appearance of Agia. I think all this can be accounted for without violating anything we know about Urth. Stretching it, maybe, when I propose a ruthless "facedancer" ship's robot in a woman's skin.

Responds to David Stockhoff's suggestion that Agia might be a robot, which would resolve the problem of twins staying close in age under time travel. The post is a social quip, saying the poster has watched the robot-Agia discussion skeptically and that Stockhoff sounds no crazier than anyone else in it. It offers no textual evidence or argument.
chats · theories: Agia is a robot

2010-06-18 Ryan Dunn replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Agia, the missing paracoita, is a robot, building on David Stockhoff's idea of a ruthless facedancer ship's robot in a woman's skin. Cites Hethor's and Severian's descriptions of her soft hands, sun-warmed flesh and golden-skinned rent, plus Hethor's vow to avenge his poppet, as evidence.
supports · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is a robot; Agia is a facedancer ship's robot in a woman's skin

Okay, I think we're on the same page. If we agree, than there IS a woman on the loose in BotNS, who is a robot.

Otherwise, I don't think Wolfe would have given us SO MUCH description of this missing paracoita.

.

HETHOR: "How soft her hands were, her little hands."

SEVERIAN: "The slender figure and delicate features that seemed incongruent in [Agilus] were compelling in her."

.

HETHOR: "... that flesh that always felt sun-warmed."

SEVERIAN: "... as I watched her, the sun touched a rent just below her waist, turning the skin there to palest gold."

.

Hethor also vows to crush any who may have his poppet ("Let h-h-hooks be buried in the hands that took her!"), and then proceeds to (under urgings from Agia herself) sick his monsters on Severian.

Just sayin'.

...ryan

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that Agia is a robot and that Hethor's motives tie her to the Shadow, so the reader must match scattered clues to solve the mystery Severian leaves open. Points to Hethor's descriptions of Agia's soft, sun-warmed flesh and Severian's own words about her, and recalls an unnamed small female attacker aboard the Ship in The Urth of the New Sun.
proposes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is a robot; Agia uses Hethor to attack Severian; Agia is the unnamed attacker on the Ship

Yep. Hethor presents the reader with a clear mystery which Severian does not solve, but he does present us with lots of information about other, seemingly unrelated people and events. The trick is to match the clues to the mystery.

Hethor's motives are pretty important to Shadow and Agia figures in those motives. She uses Hethor to attack Severian. She's not just a shopkeeper's brother.

I recall a passage of UotNS in which an unnamed small female person attacks Severian on the Ship, and I could never figure out who that was. Does anyone know what I'm talking about?

2010-06-18 António Pedro Marques replying to Ryan Dunn #

Responds to Ryan Dunn's reading of the word 'betrayed' in Severian's description of Agia's blow, accepting that a disadvantageous-position sense is possible while admitting uncertainty about English usage. Grounds this in the passage where Agia stabs up and her blade's strength shows through the shutter, not in any claim about robots.
chats · close-textual-reading

Ryan Dunn wrote (18-06-2010 16:44):

Not necessarily - 'betrayed' as in the way something you rely on ends up being counterproductive. In this case, putting her in a disavatageous position. Well, I supoose that's a possible reading, at least. I don't know, I'm no anglo.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Ryan Dunn wrote (18-06-2010 16:44):
> ...
> "Women and tailors hold the blade beneath the hand, according to the
> proverb, but Agia stabbed up to open the tripes and catch the heart from
> below, like an accomplished assassin. I turned only just in time to
> block her blade with the shutter, and the point drove through the wood to
> show a glint of steel. The very strength of her blow betrayed her."
>
> (Severian on Agia in SotL, Chap. 15, He Is Ahead of You!)
>
> .
>
> The strength betrayed what? That she was not a woman?

Not necessarily - 'betrayed' as in the way something you rely on ends up
being counterproductive. In this case, putting her in a disavatageous 
position. Well, I supoose that's a possible reading, at least. I don't know, 
I'm no anglo.

2010-06-18 Mo Holkar / UKG replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that Agia's blow with her knife was so strong that the blade stuck in wood, letting Severian wrench it free, and that her claim that she and Agilus were 'the same flesh' tells against reading her as a robot. Responds to Ryan Dunn's reading of her as betraying Severian by putting herself at a disadvantage, and to a remark about the reading being possible.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is not a robot

That is my reading -- because her blow was so strong, her knife got caught in the wood and so Severian was able to wrench it from her grasp. (And I am an anglo :-)

A few paras above this, she says that she and Agilus "were the same flesh", which would be a strange expression for a purported robot -- even a lying one.

Mo

2010-06-18 Ryan Dunn replying to António Pedro Marques #

Asks whether Agia is a robot or artificial being, floating parallels with Ossipago and Hethor's monologue and wondering who the girl in the box is. Responds to António Pedro Marques's post, which said Agia is the key figure yet little known and that Wolfe's hints may point to something other than robothood.
questions · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is a robot; Agia may be an archangelic figure

You know, Agia does have a knack for bending people to her will, whether that be Severian, Hethor, the Vodalani, etc. The aureole suggests something holy, perhaps a symbolic moment, perhaps some archangelic aspect?

If Ossipago is robot and not cacogen (I believe he is, isn't he), are there common clues connecting him and Agia? Or maybe just observations worthy of comparison (in the way there are between Hethor and Jonas, as star sailors).

We DO agree that Agia is one of the more important characters in the book, no doubt. But try as I may, I can't shake the notion that Hethor's monologue was not just a demonstration by Wolfe to show off his writing chops. Who is the girl in the box?????????

...ryan

Whole message, with quotes · original
On Jun 18, 2010, at 6:17 PM, Ant?nio Pedro Marques wrote:

> I just find that Agia is the single most important character in the whole story and precious little is known about her. Maybe Wolfe's hints are not in the sense of her being a robot, but something else - though just what I can't guess.
> 
> - She is there almost from the beginning until the end - even as Severian becomes Autarch, she becomes the Autarch's antagonist, seemingly out of nothing - what's she got to do with the Vodelarii??
> 
> - Compare Agia's omnipresence to Thecla's, who Severian is always mentioning, yet barely appears in the story in person - by contrast, Agia has a will of her own to remain relevant.
> 
> - Why the obsession with Severian? He killed her brother/lover, ok, but it's not like it was undeserved, and he did it merely as an instrument of Law - not to mention that A&A went to great lengths to entrap Severian before he did anything - can anyone believe that the twins' usual business was killing unwary travellers in avern duels?
> 
> The fact is that Agia's whole purpose from beginning to end seems to be to work as an antagonist to Severian. To me this suggests that the character is much more important and mysterious that it seems at first sight, and that the oddities about her beg for some grand explanation. Iow, I don't think the odd hints point to her being a robot, but rather to something much more extraordinary (whether part of it is being a robot or not, and bearing in mind that pretty much everything in Wolfe's books is extraordinary, both in etymological and pedestrian sense). Just what, I have absokutely no idea at all. I certainly don't think she's the Devil, she's not that powerful.



You know, Agia does have a knack for bending people to her will, whether that be Severian, Hethor, the Vodalani, etc. The aureole suggests something holy, perhaps a symbolic moment, perhaps some archangelic aspect?

If Ossipago is robot and not cacogen (I believe he is, isn't he), are there common clues connecting him and Agia? Or maybe just observations worthy of comparison (in the way there are between Hethor and Jonas, as star sailors).

We DO agree that Agia is one of the more important characters in the book, no doubt. But try as I may, I can't shake the notion that Hethor's monologue was not just a demonstration by Wolfe to show off his writing chops. Who is the girl in the box?????????

...ryan

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that Agia is a pretender to the throne of Urth who killed Vodalus and took his place, and suggests her name meaning "holy" supports this. Grounds the claim in quoted lines from The Urth of the New Sun about her spiritual yearning and in the lucivee wound that resembles a tiger's claw weapon, while agreeing with Ryan Dunn about her appearance.
proposes · close-textual-reading · source-and-allusion · theories: Agia is a pretender who killed Vodalus; Agia took Vodalus's place as a female Vodalus

Incidentally, "Agia" means "holy." So there you are.

I take her as a kind of pretender to the throne of Urth. She killed Vodalus and took his place:

Agia, whom I might have loved, has chosen instead to become a female Vodalus, taking all that lives most fully in humanity as her opponent.

Also, from UotNS:

Perhaps it was some third thing, sensed unconsciously; but I felt again as I had when, so much younger, I had encountered Agia?a desire so strong that it seemed more spiritual than any faith, its flesh burned away in the heat of its own yearning.

Um...

Also, the lucivee with which she tore Severian's cheek is named after a lynx, yet it is clearly what we call the "tiger's claw," supposedly a ninja weapon.

What was somebody saying about Agia being an assassin? (And I agree with Ryan---she is Asian in appearance.)

2010-06-18 Ryan Dunn replying to David Stockhoff #

Proposes that Agilus, not Agia, is the robot, citing his obsession with Jovinian steel and the image Agia has taken on. Replies to David Stockhoff's claim that Agia's change needs explaining, and says Hethor's visit to the rag shop is a misdirect that bothers him, so one of the two shop stories must be a lie.
proposes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Agilus is the robot, not Agia

Agilus is the obvious suspect here. The one who obsesses over Jovinian steel. The one whose image she has assumed. The problem is just the misdirect with Hethor coming to their rag shop, which we are to assume Agia and Agilus inherited from their mother. Either that or simply take that story as a lie, and Hethor's as a truth.

I don't hate the idea, it just bugs me.

...ryan

2010-06-18 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that Agia and Agilus are a rival pair of gnostic true-Man and robot figures whose holy, pseudoincestuous union opposes Severian, and that Agia later becomes Vodalus. Builds the case through numbered points on alchemy, Agia's facedancing and sentience, and her escape from the lemon-wood box, and retracts earlier speculations about time travel and Gunnie or Ida.
proposes · theology-and-religion · allegory-and-typology · theories: Agia is a robot who becomes holy and rival Christ figure; Agia becomes Vodalus after killing Hethor; Agia and Agilus are alchemical union opposing Severian

Try this on. It's quite a bit of backstory for Wolfe keep to himself, but it makes some sense.

(1) Agia and Agilus represent the alchemical union of Man and twin sister in Love.

(2) Because They are in love, this alchemical union is also holy (her name; the aureole), thus not just alchemical but gnostic.

(3) Agilus is a rival True Man, probably a lot better than Severian in many ways. Together they represent a different path for the universe, one that leads to a new universe, but not to a new universe of new hope for Man. I don't think the reason for this can lie just in Agilus, but rather---necessarily---in Their union and pseudoincestuous love. Agia is just as good an example of Robot, and They click.

(4) Thus they oppose Severian, who is the true Man who will unite with his sister and create a new universe of new hope for Man.

(5) Agia was some kind of intelligent Asian facedancer/sex-doll ninja on the ship. (A weird skill set, but also a cliched one, which makes it fair fodder for Wolfean mythical analysis. I suspect the facedancing is more for sex than for killing---that she is specialized for sex, not killing.) Hethor only controlled her because of his powers; he probably acquired her through his powers.

(6) She escaped the lemon-wood box, or else someone released her. She met Agilus and fell in Love. She made herself in His image. She awakened, or rather Awakened, since she was sentient already, but now she is more than that. Now she knows who and what she is and what she can become: she is holy in her Love. (Severian catches glimpses of this.)

(7) Too bad she has no scruples whatsoever, but then she's an assassin. Remind you of anyone? Yep, Agia would happily kill the world to spawn a new one under her holy rule; so would have Agilus. (Call it AEgypt.) Possibly the Heirodules even considered them rivals of Severian. perhaps planning to Test them if he did not kill Agilus.

It may seem odd for this transformation to happen to a robot, but it doesn't matter what she was, only what she becomes. Remember that robots are people in Wolfe's thinking, capable of good and evil regardless of their base, Man-made, subcreated origin. If Man can be god, why not robot too? What more proof of God's reality---what more of a miracle---than for a robot to be another rival false gnostic Christ figure? And she does suffer, justly, when Agilus dies.

How cool is it that robots (rather a robot-human couple) maybe even had a shot at becoming Conciliator? And aren't the Heirodules created? Obviously robots could indeed have supplanted humans if they were truly better and passed the Test---didn't that already more or less happen in a previous universe? So there is the answer to the excellent question, "So what if Agia is a robot?"

(8) She resists Hethor, who pursues her. Enter Severian, who burns spiritually for her. She probably doesn't see who he is at all, just that he is a good target, but maybe she does. After Agilus is beheaded, she masters Hethor and goes after Severian. She fails to kill Severian, but becomes Vodalus, rebellious subruler of the Commonwealth in her own right. Note that she doesn't ally with the Ascians, "once she sees them." She's not one of them at all.

I retract any speculations that Agia was a significant time-traveler or that she was allied with Gunnie or Ida. Also, that Agilus was not human.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Try this on. It's quite a bit of backstory for Wolfe keep to himself, 
but it makes some sense.

(1) Agia and Agilus represent the alchemical union of Man and twin 
sister in Love.

(2) Because They are in love, this alchemical union is also holy (her 
name; the aureole), thus not just alchemical but gnostic.

(3) Agilus is a rival True Man, probably a lot better than Severian in 
many ways. Together they represent a different path for the universe, 
one that leads to a new universe, but not to a new universe of new hope 
for Man. I don't think the reason for this can lie just in Agilus, but 
rather---necessarily---in Their union and pseudoincestuous love. Agia is 
just as good an example of Robot, and They click.

(4) Thus they oppose Severian, who is the true Man who will unite with 
his sister and create a new universe of new hope for Man.

(5) Agia was some kind of intelligent Asian facedancer/sex-doll ninja on 
the ship. (A weird skill set, but also a cliched one, which makes it 
fair fodder for Wolfean mythical analysis. I suspect the facedancing is 
more for sex than for killing---that she is specialized for sex, not 
killing.) Hethor only controlled her because of his powers; he probably 
acquired her through his powers.

(6) She escaped the lemon-wood box, or else someone released her. She 
met Agilus and fell in Love. She made herself in His image. She 
awakened, or rather Awakened, since she was sentient already, but now 
she is more than that. Now she knows who and what she is and what she 
can become: she is holy in her Love. (Severian catches glimpses of this.)

(7) Too bad she has no scruples whatsoever, but then she's an assassin. 
Remind you of anyone? Yep, Agia would happily kill the world to spawn a 
new one under her holy rule; so would have Agilus. (Call it AEgypt.) 
Possibly the Heirodules even considered them rivals of Severian. perhaps 
planning to Test them if he did not kill Agilus.

It may seem odd for this transformation to happen to a robot, but it 
doesn't matter what she was, only what she becomes. Remember that robots 
are people in Wolfe's thinking, capable of good and evil regardless of 
their base, Man-made, subcreated origin. If Man can be god, why not 
robot too? What more proof of God's reality---what more of a 
miracle---than for a robot to be another rival false gnostic Christ 
figure? And she does suffer, justly, when Agilus dies.

How cool is it that robots (rather a robot-human couple) maybe even had 
a shot at becoming Conciliator? And aren't the Heirodules created? 
Obviously robots could indeed have supplanted humans if they were truly 
better and passed the Test---didn't that already more or less happen in 
a previous universe? So there is the answer to the excellent question, 
"So what if Agia is a robot?"

(8) She resists Hethor, who pursues her. Enter Severian, who burns 
spiritually for her. She probably doesn't see who he is at all, just 
that he is a good target, but maybe she does. After Agilus is beheaded, 
she masters Hethor and goes after Severian. She fails to kill Severian, 
but becomes Vodalus, rebellious subruler of the Commonwealth in her own 
right. Note that she doesn't ally with the Ascians, "once she sees 
them." She's not one of them at all.

I retract any speculations that Agia was a significant time-traveler or 
that she was allied with Gunnie or Ida. Also, that Agilus was not human.

2010-06-18 Ryan Dunn replying to David Stockhoff #

Ryan Dunn responds to David Stockhoff's alchemical and gnostic reading of Agia and Agilus, saying he finds the idea of them not being birth twins intriguing and plausible. He suggests Agia, as an escapee, would have reason to change her identity in case Hethor found her again.
supports · puzzle-solving · theology-and-religion · theories: Agia and Agilus are not birth twins; Agia is the escapee who changed her identity

Man that's deep. But I found myself nodding a lot. Another consideration...

If she is the escapee, it makes perfect sense for her to want to change her identity, in case Hethor ever caught back up with her.

I find the notion that these two are not birth twins not only intriguing but fully plausible.

Thanks for this spirited debate and discussion, truly an awesome group of minds on here. Hopefully it wasn't too much of a retread for some.

...ryan

2010-06-18 Roy C. Lackey replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Agia cannot be a chem (artificial person) and therefore is human, marshalling textual evidence against David Stockhoff's robot theory. Cites the chem strength ratio, Silk's comment that Agia was too heavy to lift, Agia eating and drinking, Severian's rough handling of her, and Inire's interrogation and command of her in The Citadel of the Autarch.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is not a chem; Agia is human rather than robot

I have found that facts seldom get in the way of fancied theories, but here are a few.

Male chems (soldiers like Hammerstone) are taller, heavier than fem chems, and 5.25 times stronger. (That number comes from Marble, CALDE, chap. 8, p-284.) Silk said that she was too heavy for him to lift (ibid., chap. 4, p-131).

Chems don't eat or drink. Severian pushed Agia into a chair when she had a belly full of wine (SHADOW, chap. XXV). He also slapped her down, "My hand caught her just below the ear and she went sprawling, . . . " (ibid., chap. XXVII). He easily overpowered her outside the mine of the man-apes and lifted her off her feet by her hair (CLAW, chap. VII). There is no way for Agia to be a chem. Agilus's execution speaks for itself, as others have pointed out.

Jonas was not a chem. Sev picked him up in the Antechamber: "I carried him back (finding him astonishingly light) and when I . . ." (CLAW, chap. XVI). Silk and Sev were both young, strong lads.

Inire interrogated Agia and put her in command of Vodalus's rebels (CITADEL, chap. XXXV). I think that he, as a hierodule, could tell a doll from a robot from a human woman. A woman is what he called her in his letter.

-Roy

Whole message, with quotes · original

are a few.

2010-06-18 António Pedro Marques replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Agia is the most important and least explained character in The Book of the New Sun, suspecting her oddities point to something more extraordinary than being a robot, though the poster cannot say what. Grounds this in her persistence from start to finish, her role as Severian's antagonist, and the contrast with Thecla, and questions why she pursues Severian given that Severian killed her brother for lawful reasons.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is something more extraordinary than a robot

I just find that Agia is the single most important character in the whole story and precious little is known about her. Maybe Wolfe's hints are not in the sense of her being a robot, but something else - though just what I can't guess.

- She is there almost from the beginning until the end - even as Severian becomes Autarch, she becomes the Autarch's antagonist, seemingly out of nothing - what's she got to do with the Vodelarii??

- Compare Agia's omnipresence to Thecla's, who Severian is always mentioning, yet barely appears in the story in person - by contrast, Agia has a will of her own to remain relevant.

- Why the obsession with Severian? He killed her brother/lover, ok, but it's not like it was undeserved, and he did it merely as an instrument of Law - not to mention that A&A went to great lengths to entrap Severian before he did anything - can anyone believe that the twins' usual business was killing unwary travellers in avern duels?

The fact is that Agia's whole purpose from beginning to end seems to be to work as an antagonist to Severian. To me this suggests that the character is much more important and mysterious that it seems at first sight, and that the oddities about her beg for some grand explanation. Iow, I don't think the odd hints point to her being a robot, but rather to something much more extraordinary (whether part of it is being a robot or not, and bearing in mind that pretty much everything in Wolfe's books is extraordinary, both in etymological and pedestrian sense). Just what, I have absokutely no idea at all. I certainly don't think she's the Devil, she's not that powerful.

Whole message, with quotes · original

2010-06-19 Josh Geller replying to Roy C. Lackey #

Whole message, with quotes · original
She's a woman, whatever she is.

Nothing you say here makes me doubt this theory.

.


> I have found that facts seldom get in the way of fancied theories, but
> here
> are a few.
>
> Male chems (soldiers like Hammerstone) are taller, heavier than fem chems,
> and 5.25 times stronger. (That number comes from Marble, CALDE, chap. 8,
> p-284.) Silk said that she was too heavy for him to lift (ibid., chap. 4,
> p-131).
>
> Chems don't eat or drink. Severian pushed Agia into a chair when she had a
> belly full of wine (SHADOW, chap. XXV). He also slapped her down, "My hand
> caught her just below the ear and she went sprawling, . .  . " (ibid.,
> chap.
> XXVII). He easily overpowered her outside the mine of the man-apes and
> lifted her off her feet by her hair (CLAW, chap. VII). There is no way for
> Agia to be a chem. Agilus's execution speaks for itself, as others have
> pointed out.
>
> Jonas was not a chem. Sev picked him up in the Antechamber: "I carried him
> back (finding him astonishingly light) and when I . . ." (CLAW, chap.
> XVI).
> Silk and Sev were both young, strong lads.
>
> Inire interrogated Agia and put her in command of Vodalus's rebels
> (CITADEL,
> chap. XXXV). I think that he, as a hierodule, could tell a doll from a
> robot
> from a human woman. A woman is what he called her in his letter.
>
> -Roy

2010-06-19 Ryan Dunn replying to Roy C. Lackey #

Argues, playfully, that Agia is the genicon from the lemon-wood box and that Hethor's sexual use of a lighter bot points toward her, dismissing Roy Lackey's factual objections as spoiling the fun. Responds to Roy C. Lackey, who cited chem strength and weight figures and Agia's wine-drinking scene to argue she is not a robot.
disputes · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is the genicon from the lemon-wood box

Aww... you're spoiling all the fun!

As for Hethor, I'd think he'd want a lighter sex bot (genicon, he says) than a chem while frolicking in bed. Someone he could overpower easily, should it come to that.

Have you any thoughts as to whether we should be looking for the genicon from the lemon-wood box in the text?

If yes, where to look? I'm following the clues, and they keep leading me to Agia. That's all I'm saying.

...ryan

2010-06-19 David Stockhoff replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Agia is literally holy as a Heirodule directly created by the gods, not a robot, and presents a theory that her and Agilus's twinhood and holiness fit gnostic and alchemical patterns in the Book of the New Sun. Defends the view by claiming the theory explains Agia's ninja traits, the doll in the box, her eye colours, and Severian's incestuous tendencies, and by answering Roy's objections.
proposes · theology-and-religion · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is a Heirodule, not a robot; Agia and Agilus are twins in sacred incest; Agia was made as an assassin, not a sex toy; Severian's light created the aureole around Agia and Agilus

Let me add a point or two to address Roy's observations.

(9) Agia is literally holy, in that she is a Heirodule, directly created by the gods.

(10) As an assassin, she is the anti-Ossipago. If you gave Ossipago a shove, I bet you'd hear the mason's hammer striking stone. There's no reason to compare her to chems, creations of Typhon.

There bound to be some misses here, but this theory uses known principles to predict what we know we don't know. It draws from plentiful pulp and SF cliches, including some playful ones, and directly relates to the gnostic themes of TBotNS. It uses the sacred incest central to gnosticism/alchemy and explains why Agia and Agilus are twins, and confirms the significance of Severian's incestuous tendencies. It explains why Agia, obviously an extremely important character, is so important. It even explains why she is an Asian hottie ninja, a fact Lexicon Urthis overlooks. It identifies the doll in the box and explains her purple eyes and Agia's brown ones. It relies heavily on Wolfe's near-obsession with robots and half-humans, and ties in with the theme of base matter trapped in a subcreation searching for godhead and the next universe. It almost completely disregards established differences between robots and humans, because what matters in religion is the soul. It's a thoroughly Wolfean scheme.

Let me know if there's anything it doesn't explain.

Also, after looking back a bit, I think Agia was made primarily as an assassin, not as a sex toy. Hethor made a toy of her, and she is capable of sex because she is an assassin, not the reverse. If this and the above are true, then there was probably never a chance of A&A being Tested; that was just speculation anyway. It's difficult to see the gods' motives in creating her and too easy to make wild guesses.

I wonder if it was Severian's own light that was the source of the aureole around A&A.

Let me add a point or two to address Roy's observations.

(9) Agia is literally holy, in that she is a Heirodule, directly created 
by the gods.

(10) As an assassin, she is the anti-Ossipago. If you gave Ossipago a 
shove, I bet you'd hear the mason's hammer striking stone. There's no 
reason to compare her to chems, creations of Typhon.

There bound to be some misses here, but this theory uses known 
principles to predict what we know we don't know. It draws from 
plentiful pulp and SF cliches, including some playful ones, and directly 
relates to the gnostic themes of TBotNS. It uses the sacred incest 
central to gnosticism/alchemy and explains why Agia and Agilus are 
twins, and confirms the significance of Severian's incestuous 
tendencies. It explains why Agia, obviously an extremely important 
character, is so important. It even explains why she is an Asian hottie 
ninja, a fact Lexicon Urthis overlooks. It identifies the doll in the 
box and explains her purple eyes and Agia's brown ones. It relies 
heavily on Wolfe's near-obsession with robots and half-humans, and ties 
in with the theme of base matter trapped in a subcreation searching for 
godhead and the next universe. It almost completely disregards 
established differences between robots and humans, because what matters 
in religion is the soul. It's a thoroughly Wolfean scheme.

Let me know if there's anything it doesn't explain.

Also, after looking back a bit, I think Agia was made primarily as an 
assassin, not as a sex toy. Hethor made a toy of her, and she is capable 
of sex because she is an assassin, not the reverse. If this and the 
above are true, then there was probably never a chance of A&A being 
Tested; that was just speculation anyway. It's difficult to see the 
gods' motives in creating her and too easy to make wild guesses.

I wonder if it was Severian's own light that was the source of the 
aureole around A&A.

David Stockhoff wrote:
> Try this on. It's quite a bit of backstory for Wolfe keep to himself, 
> but it makes some sense.
>
> (1) Agia and Agilus represent the alchemical union of Man and twin 
> sister in Love.
>
> (2) Because They are in love, this alchemical union is also holy (her 
> name; the aureole), thus not just alchemical but gnostic.
>
> (3) Agilus is a rival True Man, probably a lot better than Severian in 
> many ways. Together they represent a different path for the universe, 
> one that leads to a new universe, but not to a new universe of new 
> hope for Man. I don't think the reason for this can lie just in 
> Agilus, but rather---necessarily---in Their union and pseudoincestuous 
> love. Agia is just as good an example of Robot, and They click.
>
> (4) Thus they oppose Severian, who is the true Man who will unite with 
> his sister and create a new universe of new hope for Man.
>
> (5) Agia was some kind of intelligent Asian facedancer/sex-doll ninja 
> on the ship. (A weird skill set, but also a cliched one, which makes 
> it fair fodder for Wolfean mythical analysis. I suspect the 
> facedancing is more for sex than for killing---that she is specialized 
> for sex, not killing.) Hethor only controlled her because of his 
> powers; he probably acquired her through his powers.
>
> (6) She escaped the lemon-wood box, or else someone released her. She 
> met Agilus and fell in Love. She made herself in His image. She 
> awakened, or rather Awakened, since she was sentient already, but now 
> she is more than that. Now she knows who and what she is and what she 
> can become: she is holy in her Love. (Severian catches glimpses of this.)
>
> (7) Too bad she has no scruples whatsoever, but then she's an 
> assassin. Remind you of anyone? Yep, Agia would happily kill the world 
> to spawn a new one under her holy rule; so would have Agilus. (Call it 
> AEgypt.) Possibly the Heirodules even considered them rivals of 
> Severian. perhaps planning to Test them if he did not kill Agilus.
>
> It may seem odd for this transformation to happen to a robot, but it 
> doesn't matter what she was, only what she becomes. Remember that 
> robots are people in Wolfe's thinking, capable of good and evil 
> regardless of their base, Man-made, subcreated origin. If Man can be 
> god, why not robot too? What more proof of God's reality---what more 
> of a miracle---than for a robot to be another rival false gnostic 
> Christ figure? And she does suffer, justly, when Agilus dies.
>
> How cool is it that robots (rather a robot-human couple) maybe even 
> had a shot at becoming Conciliator? And aren't the Heirodules created? 
> Obviously robots could indeed have supplanted humans if they were 
> truly better and passed the Test---didn't that already more or less 
> happen in a previous universe? So there is the answer to the excellent 
> question, "So what if Agia is a robot?"
>
> (8) She resists Hethor, who pursues her. Enter Severian, who burns 
> spiritually for her. She probably doesn't see who he is at all, just 
> that he is a good target, but maybe she does. After Agilus is 
> beheaded, she masters Hethor and goes after Severian. She fails to 
> kill Severian, but becomes Vodalus, rebellious subruler of the 
> Commonwealth in her own right. Note that she doesn't ally with the 
> Ascians, "once she sees them." She's not one of them at all.
>
> I retract any speculations that Agia was a significant time-traveler 
> or that she was allied with Gunnie or Ida. Also, that Agilus was not 
> human.
>
> Ryan Dunn wrote:
>> Agilus is the obvious suspect here. The one who obsesses over 
>> Jovinian steel. The one whose image she has assumed. The problem is 
>> just the misdirect with Hethor coming to their rag shop, which we are 
>> to assume Agia and Agilus inherited from their mother. Either that or 
>> simply take that story as a lie, and Hethor's as a truth.
>>
>> I don't hate the idea, it just bugs me.
>>
>> ...ryan
>>
