> heavily on Wolfe's near-obsession with robots and half-humans, and ties
> in with the theme of base matter trapped in a subcreation searching for
> godhead and the next universe. It almost completely disregards
> established differences between robots and humans, because what matters
> in religion is the soul. It's a thoroughly Wolfean scheme.
>
> Let me know if there's anything it doesn't explain.
>
> Also, after looking back a bit, I think Agia was made primarily as an
> assassin, not as a sex toy. Hethor made a toy of her, and she is capable
> of sex because she is an assassin, not the reverse. If this and the
> above are true, then there was probably never a chance of A&A being
> Tested; that was just speculation anyway. It's difficult to see the
> gods' motives in creating her and too easy to make wild guesses.
>
> I wonder if it was Severian's own light that was the source of the
> aureole around A&A.
>
> David Stockhoff wrote:
>> Try this on. It's quite a bit of backstory for Wolfe keep to himself,
>> but it makes some sense.
>>
>> (1) Agia and Agilus represent the alchemical union of Man and twin
>> sister in Love.
>>
>> (2) Because They are in love, this alchemical union is also holy (her
>> name; the aureole), thus not just alchemical but gnostic.
>>
>> (3) Agilus is a rival True Man, probably a lot better than Severian in
>> many ways. Together they represent a different path for the universe,
>> one that leads to a new universe, but not to a new universe of new
>> hope for Man. I don't think the reason for this can lie just in
>> Agilus, but rather---necessarily---in Their union and pseudoincestuous
>> love. Agia is just as good an example of Robot, and They click.
>>
>> (4) Thus they oppose Severian, who is the true Man who will unite with
>> his sister and create a new universe of new hope for Man.
>>
>> (5) Agia was some kind of intelligent Asian facedancer/sex-doll ninja
>> on the ship. (A weird skill set, but also a cliched one, which makes
>> it fair fodder for Wolfean mythical analysis. I suspect the
>> facedancing is more for sex than for killing---that she is specialized
>> for sex, not killing.) Hethor only controlled her because of his
>> powers; he probably acquired her through his powers.
>>
>> (6) She escaped the lemon-wood box, or else someone released her. She
>> met Agilus and fell in Love. She made herself in His image. She
>> awakened, or rather Awakened, since she was sentient already, but now
>> she is more than that. Now she knows who and what she is and what she
>> can become: she is holy in her Love. (Severian catches glimpses of
>> this.)
>>
>> (7) Too bad she has no scruples whatsoever, but then she's an
>> assassin. Remind you of anyone? Yep, Agia would happily kill the world
>> to spawn a new one under her holy rule; so would have Agilus. (Call it
>> AEgypt.) Possibly the Heirodules even considered them rivals of
>> Severian. perhaps planning to Test them if he did not kill Agilus.
>>
>> It may seem odd for this transformation to happen to a robot, but it
>> doesn't matter what she was, only what she becomes. Remember that
>> robots are people in Wolfe's thinking, capable of good and evil
>> regardless of their base, Man-made, subcreated origin. If Man can be
>> god, why not robot too? What more proof of God's reality---what more
>> of a miracle---than for a robot to be another rival false gnostic
>> Christ figure? And she does suffer, justly, when Agilus dies.
>>
>> How cool is it that robots (rather a robot-human couple) maybe even
>> had a shot at becoming Conciliator? And aren't the Heirodules created?
>> Obviously robots could indeed have supplanted humans if they were
>> truly better and passed the Test---didn't that already more or less
>> happen in a previous universe? So there is the answer to the excellent
>> question, "So what if Agia is a robot?"
>>
>> (8) She resists Hethor, who pursues her. Enter Severian, who burns
>> spiritually for her. She probably doesn't see who he is at all, just
>> that he is a good target, but maybe she does. After Agilus is
>> beheaded, she masters Hethor and goes after Severian. She fails to
>> kill Severian, but becomes Vodalus, rebellious subruler of the
>> Commonwealth in her own right. Note that she doesn't ally with the
>> Ascians, "once she sees them." She's not one of them at all.

>> I retract any speculations that Agia was a significant time-traveler

>> human.

2010-06-19 Jeff Wilson replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues Agia is no saint and that her modesty and hunger tell against her being a sexbot robot. Responds to Ryan Dunn's aureole remark by contrasting it with Thecla's dark aureole on p.70 of SHADOW, citing Agia's modesty before the Pelerines and her need to eat.
disputes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is a robot or sexbot

Bless you, man.

Agia's no saint - I'd say that given her record of sacrilege, multiple murder, and treason, she's about as unsaintly as they come, in a league with Baldanders and the woman who made furniture out of children. If the bright aureole represents anything, it's representative of contrast or inversion esp with Thecla whose hair is "a dark aureole" about p.70 of SHADOW.

Come to think of it, when she was strip-seached by the Pelerines, Agia was awfully modest, and when discovered with Agilus, she holds her gown over her nakedness. Agilus mentions her needing to eat. Why would a sexbot be modest or hungry?

2010-06-19 David Stockhoff replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that the named groups in Father Inire's letter about sedition among the Paralians are simply imperial legions, not subject peoples as Lexicon Urthus suggests. Supports this by citing the letter as a post-battle report, noting the groups appear nowhere else, and tracing Paralians, Tarentines and Antrustiones to real-world sources such as Athenian sailors and Frankish retainers.
disputes · source-and-allusion · close-textual-reading · theories: Paralians are imperial sailors and marines, not a subject people; Tarentines are mercenary cavalry Severian fought among

Father Inire writes to Severian:

There is sedition among the Paralians, which must be rooted out. The Tarentines, your Antrustiones, and the city legions?the three groups that bore the brunt of the fighting?having suffered almost as badly as the enemy. There are cohorts among them that could not muster a hundred able soldiers.

Lexicon Urthis suggests that these named groups are various subject peoples and tries to locate them. But this is plainly an error. Inire is simply giving the new Autarch a post-battle report. All these named groups are simply "imperial" legions and are mentioned nowhere else.

According to Google:

Paralians - Athenians off a ship named Paralus = Sailors and Marines. No kidding that there is sedition---Gunnie and Ida are among them.

Tarentines = Italian mercenary cavalry = mercenary cavalry, possibly those Severian fought among. "Hipparch Severian, of the Black Tarentines."

Antrustiones = foreign soldiers fighting for the the Frankish king = foreign soldiers fighting for the Autarch.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Father Inire writes to Severian:

There is sedition among the Paralians, which must be rooted out. The 
Tarentines, your Antrustiones, and the city legions?the three groups 
that bore the brunt of the fighting?having suffered almost as badly as 
the enemy. There are cohorts among them that could not muster a hundred 
able soldiers.

Lexicon Urthis suggests that these named groups are various subject 
peoples and tries to locate them. But this is plainly an error. Inire is 
simply giving the new Autarch a post-battle report. All these named 
groups are simply "imperial" legions and are mentioned nowhere else.

According to Google:

Paralians - Athenians off a ship named Paralus = Sailors and Marines. No 
kidding that there is sedition---Gunnie and Ida are among them.

Tarentines = Italian mercenary cavalry = mercenary cavalry, possibly 
those Severian fought among. "Hipparch Severian, of the Black Tarentines."

Antrustiones = foreign soldiers fighting for the the Frankish king = 
foreign soldiers fighting for the Autarch.

2010-06-19 David Stockhoff replying to brunians #

Remarks that the question of whether Agia is a sex doll or an assassin doll is a minor point, speculating that a sex doll is easier to propose without a motive. Replies to brunians, agreeing with his view while reserving judgment on his second-to-last paragraph, and wryly doubts its own point.
chats · theories: Agia is a sex doll; Agia is an assassin doll

Doll vs assassin? It's a minor point.

I suppose it's easier to propose a sex doll without a motive for it than to propose an assassin doll without a motive for it. Or maybe not.

>>> reason to compare her to chems, creations of Typhon.

>>> There bound to be some misses here, but this theory uses known
>>> principles to predict what we know we don't know. It draws from
>>> plentiful pulp and SF cliches, including some playful ones, and
>>> directly
>>> relates to the gnostic themes of TBotNS. It uses the sacred incest
>>> central to gnosticism/alchemy and explains why Agia and Agilus are
>>> twins, and confirms the significance of Severian's incestuous
>>> tendencies. It explains why Agia, obviously an extremely important
>>> character, is so important. It even explains why she is an Asian hottie
>>> ninja, a fact Lexicon Urthis overlooks. It identifies the doll in the
>>> box and explains her purple eyes and Agia's brown ones. It relies
>>> heavily on Wolfe's near-obsession with robots and half-humans, and ties
>>> in with the theme of base matter trapped in a subcreation searching for
>>> godhead and the next universe. It almost completely disregards
>>> established differences between robots and humans, because what matters
>>> in religion is the soul. It's a thoroughly Wolfean scheme.
>>>
>>> Let me know if there's anything it doesn't explain.
>>>
>>> Also, after looking back a bit, I think Agia was made primarily as an
>>> assassin, not as a sex toy. Hethor made a toy of her, and she is
>>> capable
>>> of sex because she is an assassin, not the reverse. If this and the
>>> above are true, then there was probably never a chance of A&A being
>>> Tested; that was just speculation anyway. It's difficult to see the
>>> gods' motives in creating her and too easy to make wild guesses.
>>>
>>> I wonder if it was Severian's own light that was the source of the
>>> aureole around A&A.
>>>
>>> David Stockhoff wrote:
>>>
>>>> Try this on. It's quite a bit of backstory for Wolfe keep to himself,
>>>> but it makes some sense.
>>>>
>>>> (1) Agia and Agilus represent the alchemical union of Man and twin
>>>> sister in Love.
>>>>
>>>> (2) Because They are in love, this alchemical union is also holy (her
>>>> name; the aureole), thus not just alchemical but gnostic.
>>>>
>>>> (3) Agilus is a rival True Man, probably a lot better than Severian in
>>>> many ways. Together they represent a different path for the universe,
>>>> one that leads to a new universe, but not to a new universe of new
>>>> hope for Man. I don't think the reason for this can lie just in
>>>> Agilus, but rather---necessarily---in Their union and pseudoincestuous
>>>> love. Agia is just as good an example of Robot, and They click.
>>>>
>>>> (4) Thus they oppose Severian, who is the true Man who will unite with
>>>> his sister and create a new universe of new hope for Man.
>>>>
>>>> (5) Agia was some kind of intelligent Asian facedancer/sex-doll ninja
>>>> on the ship. (A weird skill set, but also a cliched one, which makes
>>>> it fair fodder for Wolfean mythical analysis. I suspect the
>>>> facedancing is more for sex than for killing---that she is specialized
>>>> for sex, not killing.) Hethor only controlled her because of his
>>>> powers; he probably acquired her through his powers.
>>>>
>>>> (6) She escaped the lemon-wood box, or else someone released her. She
>>>> met Agilus and fell in Love. She made herself in His image. She
>>>> awakened, or rather Awakened, since she was sentient already, but now
>>>> she is more than that. Now she knows who and what she is and what she
>>>> can become: she is holy in her Love. (Severian catches glimpses of
>>>> this.)
>>>>
>>>> (7) Too bad she has no scruples whatsoever, but then she's an
>>>> assassin. Remind you of anyone? Yep, Agia would happily kill the world
>>>> to spawn a new one under her holy rule; so would have Agilus. (Call it
>>>> AEgypt.) Possibly the Heirodules even considered them rivals of
>>>> Severian. perhaps planning to Test them if he did not kill Agilus.
>>>>
>>>> It may seem odd for this transformation to happen to a robot, but it
>>>> doesn't matter what she was, only what she becomes. Remember that
>>>> robots are people in Wolfe's thinking, capable of good and evil
>>>> regardless of their base, Man-made, subcreated origin. If Man can be
>>>> god, why not robot too? What more proof of God's reality---what more
>>>> of a miracle---than for a robot to be another rival false gnostic
>>>> Christ figure? And she does suffer, justly, when Agilus dies.
>>>>
>>>> How cool is it that robots (rather a robot-human couple) maybe even
>>>> had a shot at becoming Conciliator? And aren't the Heirodules created?
>>>> Obviously robots could indeed have supplanted humans if they were
>>>> truly better and passed the Test---didn't that already more or less
>>>> happen in a previous universe? So there is the answer to the excellent
>>>> question, "So what if Agia is a robot?"
>>>>
>>>> (8) She resists Hethor, who pursues her. Enter Severian, who burns
>>>> spiritually for her. She probably doesn't see who he is at all, just
>>>> that he is a good target, but maybe she does. After Agilus is
>>>> beheaded, she masters Hethor and goes after Severian. She fails to
>>>> kill Severian, but becomes Vodalus, rebellious subruler of the
>>>> Commonwealth in her own right. Note that she doesn't ally with the
>>>> Ascians, "once she sees them." She's not one of them at all.
>>>>
>>>> I retract any speculations that Agia was a significant time-traveler
>>>> or that she was allied with Gunnie or Ida. Also, that Agilus was not
>>>> human.
>>>>
>>>> Ryan Dunn wrote:
>>>>
>>>>> Agilus is the obvious suspect here. The one who obsesses over
>>>>> Jovinian steel. The one whose image she has assumed. The problem is
>>>>> just the misdirect with Hethor coming to their rag shop, which we are
>>>>> to assume Agia and Agilus inherited from their mother. Either that or

2010-06-19 David Stockhoff replying to brunians #

Argues that Agia may be a robot, citing the Kim Lee Soong group in the Antechamber and the ninja weapons as support, while noting the eye color change and her resemblance to Agilus. Raises questions about Agilus's origins, why the figures lack Asian names, who made her, and her weight and strength.
proposes · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is an oriental robot

That makes some sense. It jives with the Kim Lee Soong group in the Antechamber. It explains the ninja weapons. It's simple.

It doesn't explain the eye color change, though perhaps that is such a minor alteration as to allow us to jettison the "facedancer" technology (she never changes gender either), except for one point. How does she resemble Agilus so closely? Is that just Severian's inability to distinguish Asians? Or is Agilus fresh off the ship too, and Nessus has not seen his kind for millennia? Why do they not have Asian names?

Who made her? How is she so light and yet so strong? Are those common sex-doll specs?

>>>>> box and explains her purple eyes and Agia's brown ones. It relies
>>>>> heavily on Wolfe's near-obsession with robots and half-humans, and
>>>>> ties
>>>>> in with the theme of base matter trapped in a subcreation searching
>>>>> for
>>>>> godhead and the next universe. It almost completely disregards
>>>>> established differences between robots and humans, because what
>>>>> matters
>>>>> in religion is the soul. It's a thoroughly Wolfean scheme.
>>>>>
>>>>> Let me know if there's anything it doesn't explain.
>>>>>
>>>>> Also, after looking back a bit, I think Agia was made primarily as an
>>>>> assassin, not as a sex toy. Hethor made a toy of her, and she is
>>>>> capable
>>>>> of sex because she is an assassin, not the reverse. If this and the
>>>>> above are true, then there was probably never a chance of A&A being
>>>>> Tested; that was just speculation anyway. It's difficult to see the
>>>>> gods' motives in creating her and too easy to make wild guesses.

>>>>>> (1) Agia and Agilus represent the alchemical union of Man and twin

>>>>>> that he is a good target, but maybe she does. After Agilus is
>>>>>> beheaded, she masters Hethor and goes after Severian. She fails to
>>>>>> kill Severian, but becomes Vodalus, rebellious subruler of the
>>>>>> Commonwealth in her own right. Note that she doesn't ally with the
>>>>>> Ascians, "once she sees them." She's not one of them at all.

2010-06-19 Jeff Wilson replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that Agia is an artificial being, citing her appearance at the Torturers' yearly pageantry, her unchanged looks across decades, and her recovery from staged decapitation. Adds that the Brotherhood of Seekers, as conservators of ancient technology, makes such a made creature plausible, extending Ryan Dunn's joking remark about Hethor's genicon and sex bot.
proposes · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is a robot

The Torturers' yearly pageantry employs a woman not seen between times, has an identical appearance over the decades, and engages in the staged artifice of not just surviving but recovering from decapitation, and in addition to being torturers and executioners, the Brotherhood of Seekers are also conservators of ancient technology....

2010-06-19 Ryan Dunn replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that Agia is a fallen-angel figure whose holy-sounding name and bright aureole are deliberate inversions, citing her escape from the stars with Hethor and the lighting in her cell. Responds to Jeff Wilson's claim that Agia is no saint, pointing to the contrast with Thecla's dark aureole in The Shadow of the Torturer.
disputes · close-textual-reading · allegory-and-typology · theories: Agia is a fallen angel figure; Agia escaped the stars with Hethor as a genicon

And yet, her name's etymology indicates "holy". Wolfe is having fun with this. She has a fallen angel quality. And back to the idea of coming from Quasar with Hethor, she literally did come from the stars and escaped.

Just twisting that dagger a bit.

And the aureole description. I'm just picturing a dark cell and a shaft of sunlight creating this halo effect around the two of them.

Wolfe is telling us something. Think of it in cinematic terms for a minute. It's not like he said her teeth were white as pearls. Or her hair a dark aureole cascading around her shoulders. This one is an actual lighting effect, something Wolfe intentionally included.

Could mean nothing, could have an archangel subtext. Could be a sardonic hint at her falling from the stars as a genicon escaped her master.

...ryan

2010-06-19 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that the Agia puzzle's racial and physical clues have a mundane explanation: centuries of mixing make Asian features common, and Agia's lightness and strength come from her slenderness, training and hardened purpose. Grounds this in Severian's comments on broad cheekbones and elongated eyes and on overcoming the reluctance to strike another person, drawing a parallel to Latro.
disputes · close-textual-reading · genre-and-craft · theories: Agia is not a robot but a human woman

After many chiliads of racial mixing from mechanized travel, there would be a much smoother distribution of "Asian" features. Severian has seen many people with broad cheekbones and elongated eyes, at least by the time of his writing.

She's light because she's a slender woman, and Severian has been trained to be able to handle even burly men. Her strength comes from her determination of purpose and her acquired disregard for human life over many con jobs and murders for profit. Was it Severian or Latro who discusses learning to overcome the common reluctance to strike another person with full force? It's the same thing here.

Whole message, with quotes · original
On 6/19/2010 8:45 AM, David Stockhoff wrote:

> minor alteration as to allow us to jettison the "facedancer" technology
> (she never changes gender either), except for one point. How does she
> resemble Agilus so closely? Is that just Severian's inability to
> distinguish Asians? Or is Agilus fresh off the ship too, and Nessus has
> not seen his kind for millennia? Why do they not have Asian names?

After many chiliads of racial mixing from mechanized travel, there would 
be a  much smoother distribution of "Asian" features. Severian has seen 
many people with broad cheekbones and elongated eyes, at least by the 
time of his writing.

> Who made her? How is she so light and yet so strong? Are those common
> sex-doll specs?

She's light because she's a slender woman, and Severian has been trained 
to be able to handle even burly men. Her strength comes from her 
determination of purpose and her acquired disregard for human life over 
many con jobs and murders for profit. Was it Severian or Latro who 
discusses learning to overcome the common reluctance to strike another 
person with full force? It's the same thing here.

2010-06-19 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that Agia is not a robot in any usual sense despite her metal head, and that she may be hiding something rather than simply modest. Responds to Ryan Dunn's and Jeff Wilson's remarks on Agia's sacrilege and character, proposing she is an assassin and noting the aureole covers the pair rather than Agia alone.
disputes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is an assassin; Agia is not a robot; Agia is hiding something

Good points. First, she's not proposed to be a "bot" in any usual sense, aside from having a metal head. And Agilus can lie, or maybe he doesn't even know what she is.

Second, she may have something to hide, vs having modesty, although modesty could just as well be "programmed." For all I know, she has a penis.

Third, you are exactly right about Agia's character. Severian is a torturer; I propose that Agia is an assassin; Brunians proposes that she is a whore. His theory works better in some ways than mine.

Fourth, the aureole is not of hair, but that's an interesting observation. More importantly, it covers the pair, not Agia. The two together are more than either of them individually.

>>>>>>> on the ship. (A weird skill set, but also a cliched one, which makes
>>>>>>> it fair fodder for Wolfean mythical analysis. I suspect the
>>>>>>> facedancing is more for sex than for killing---that she is
>>>>>>> specialized
>>>>>>> for sex, not killing.) Hethor only controlled her because of his
>>>>>>> powers; he probably acquired her through his powers.
>>>>>>>
>>>>>>> (6) She escaped the lemon-wood box, or else someone released her.
>>>>>>> She
>>>>>>> met Agilus and fell in Love. She made herself in His image. She
>>>>>>> awakened, or rather Awakened, since she was sentient already, but
>>>>>>> now
>>>>>>> she is more than that. Now she knows who and what she is and what
>>>>>>> she
>>>>>>> can become: she is holy in her Love. (Severian catches glimpses of
>>>>>>> this.)
>>>>>>>
>>>>>>> (7) Too bad she has no scruples whatsoever, but then she's an
>>>>>>> assassin. Remind you of anyone? Yep, Agia would happily kill the
>>>>>>> world
>>>>>>> to spawn a new one under her holy rule; so would have Agilus. (Call
>>>>>>> it
>>>>>>> AEgypt.) Possibly the Heirodules even considered them rivals of
>>>>>>> Severian. perhaps planning to Test them if he did not kill Agilus.
>>>>>>>
>>>>>>> It may seem odd for this transformation to happen to a robot, but it
>>>>>>> doesn't matter what she was, only what she becomes. Remember that
>>>>>>> robots are people in Wolfe's thinking, capable of good and evil
>>>>>>> regardless of their base, Man-made, subcreated origin. If Man can be
>>>>>>> god, why not robot too? What more proof of God's reality---what more
>>>>>>> of a miracle---than for a robot to be another rival false gnostic
>>>>>>> Christ figure? And she does suffer, justly, when Agilus dies.
>>>>>>>
>>>>>>> How cool is it that robots (rather a robot-human couple) maybe even
>>>>>>> had a shot at becoming Conciliator? And aren't the Heirodules
>>>>>>> created?
>>>>>>> Obviously robots could indeed have supplanted humans if they were
>>>>>>> truly better and passed the Test---didn't that already more or less
>>>>>>> happen in a previous universe? So there is the answer to the
>>>>>>> excellent
>>>>>>> question, "So what if Agia is a robot?"
>>>>>>>
>>>>>>> (8) She resists Hethor, who pursues her. Enter Severian, who burns
>>>>>>> spiritually for her. She probably doesn't see who he is at all, just
>>>>>>> that he is a good target, but maybe she does. After Agilus is
>>>>>>> beheaded, she masters Hethor and goes after Severian. She fails to
>>>>>>> kill Severian, but becomes Vodalus, rebellious subruler of the
>>>>>>> Commonwealth in her own right. Note that she doesn't ally with the
>>>>>>> Ascians, "once she sees them." She's not one of them at all.
>>>>>>>
>>>>>>> I retract any speculations that Agia was a significant time-traveler
>>>>>>> or that she was allied with Gunnie or Ida. Also, that Agilus was not
>>>>>>> human.
>>>>>>>

2010-06-19 David Stockhoff replying to brunians #

Argues that Agia cannot be a fully functional android, since such a being would not boast of her strength and nuclear power cell as she does in the text. Responds to brunians's suggestion that Agia is a robot by using her boasting as evidence against the reading.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is a robot or android

And if she's supposed to be a fully functional android, it would hardly do for her to go around boasting of her strength and her nuclear power cell.

Whole message, with quotes · original
And if she's supposed to be a fully functional android, it would hardly 
do for her to go around boasting of her strength and her nuclear power cell.

>>>>>>> Let me add a point or two to address Roy's observations.
>>>>>>>
>>>>>>> (9) Agia is literally holy, in that she is a Heirodule, directly
>>>>>>> created
>>>>>>> by the gods.
>>>>>>>
>>>>>>> (10) As an assassin, she is the anti-Ossipago. If you gave Ossipago
>>>>>>> a
>>>>>>> shove, I bet you'd hear the mason's hammer striking stone. There's
>>>>>>> no
>>>>>>> reason to compare her to chems, creations of Typhon.
>>>>>>>
>>>>>>> There bound to be some misses here, but this theory uses known
>>>>>>> principles to predict what we know we don't know. It draws from
>>>>>>> plentiful pulp and SF cliches, including some playful ones, and
>>>>>>> directly
>>>>>>> relates to the gnostic themes of TBotNS. It uses the sacred incest
>>>>>>> central to gnosticism/alchemy and explains why Agia and Agilus are
>>>>>>> twins, and confirms the significance of Severian's incestuous
>>>>>>> tendencies. It explains why Agia, obviously an extremely important
>>>>>>> character, is so important. It even explains why she is an Asian
>>>>>>> hottie
>>>>>>> ninja, a fact Lexicon Urthis overlooks. It identifies the doll in
>>>>>>> the
>>>>>>> box and explains her purple eyes and Agia's brown ones. It relies
>>>>>>> heavily on Wolfe's near-obsession with robots and half-humans, and
>>>>>>> ties
>>>>>>> in with the theme of base matter trapped in a subcreation searching
>>>>>>> for
>>>>>>> godhead and the next universe. It almost completely disregards
>>>>>>> established differences between robots and humans, because what
>>>>>>> matters
>>>>>>> in religion is the soul. It's a thoroughly Wolfean scheme.
>>>>>>>
>>>>>>> Let me know if there's anything it doesn't explain.
>>>>>>>
>>>>>>> Also, after looking back a bit, I think Agia was made primarily as
>>>>>>> an
>>>>>>> assassin, not as a sex toy. Hethor made a toy of her, and she is
>>>>>>> capable
>>>>>>> of sex because she is an assassin, not the reverse. If this and the

>>>>>>>> name; the aureole), thus not just alchemical but gnostic.

>>>>>>>> Severian

2010-06-19 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues against the idea that Agia is a robot, citing the sound of a skull striking stone compared with a baseball beaning and noting that her modesty follows her being seen naked. Rebuts Stockhoff's assassin reading by saying her killings serve only her and her brother, and reinterprets the aureole and aureate-face passages as dust, mist, or skin tone rather than a halo.
disputes · close-textual-reading · genre-and-craft · theories: Agia is a robot; Agia is an assassin; Agia's aureole is dust or mist

Here's a video of a pitcher getting beaned. Right after the crack of the bat, there's a *tok* of the ball-skull collision. IMO the bat's crack is sharper than the skull's *tok* , which is still sharper than the *thump* of meaty limbs being struck on the floor which doubtless we are all familiar with. The ball hits faster than Agia's head would have hit the wall, but the stone wall would be much more rigid than a baseball.

http://www.youtube.com/watch?v=auss-QFCor4&feature=PlayList&p=4C9AAD000C45EE30&playnext_from=PL&playnext=1&index=18

http://preview.tinyurl.com/2dy5gd2

I live in a fairly rural area, I suppose I could round up a cattle skull, put a wig on it, and record it smacking it into stone surface.

This is modesty *after* she has been seen completely naked. And why would anyone program a sexbot to cast runic curses?

I do not see that Agia is an assassin, her murders do not appear to serve anyone's purposes outside hers and her brothers.

Rereading about their "aureate faces" and "a narrow beam of sunlight that had given his head, and Agia's, an aureole a few moments before", it could be the dust and fibers from the straw on the floor, the way mist makes an aureole around the moon's sunlit face.

http://www.lpod.org/?p=702

or since "aureate" can also eman decorated with gold, there might be no actual fringe of light, but instead this would be another reference to their skin tone, which Sev has already likened to gold where the sun glints from Agia's skin through a rent in her ragged gown.

I'm not saying this doesn't count as a subtextual halo, of course.

That's not the same as a sexbot - "sleeping" immobile in a box is a strong advertisement that you are not really a person.

2010-06-19 Josh Geller replying to Jeff Wilson #

Disputes David Stockhoff's claim that mechanized travel has smoothed out racial differences in the far future, arguing that history is cyclical rather than linear. Responds to a quoted passage about Agia's features and strength by rejecting the modern assumption that humanity's mixing is unprecedented, though it gives no textual evidence from the books.
disputes

This is a false belief, about the smoothing of the races.

It assumes, as many modern false beliefs do, that history is not cyclical; tbat humans were smart animals and there weren't many of them for 1,990,000 years, and that in the past ten thousand years we have done all of this for the first time.

In other words, this is what most of you believe, but it is not what I believe, and it is not what Gene believes.

.

2010-06-19 Roy C. Lackey replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Agia is not a robot or chem, rebutting posts that linked her to chems through metal bodies and fiber-optic wires. Grounds this in the Book of the New Sun text: Severian strikes her with his sword and hand and notes no metal-on-metal sound or anything unusual about her head.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is not a chem or robot

A link to chems was made, I suppose because chems have metal bodies, which led to posts about chems and metal heads and black fluid and fiber-optic wires. That was the only reason I bothered to shoot down the notion that she and/or Agilus might be chems.

This whole thread began because of one line about the sound her head made when it struck the stone wall of Agilus's cell. Outside the mine of the man-apes, Severian had her down on the ground with his boot on her neck. When she began to struggle again, he rapped her with the flat of his sword. He doesn't actually say that he struck her head with it, but the blow quieted her, so that seems most likely. Wherever he struck her with the blade, he also failed to note any metal-on-metal sound.

Also, as I quoted, he struck her just below the ear with his hand, when they were in Agilus's cell. If there was anything unusual about the head he struck, he also failed to note that.

And I see no real connection between Agia and Hethor's lost sex doll.

-Roy

Whole message, with quotes · original
David Stockhoff wrote:
> No one is saying Agia is a chem.

A link to chems was made, I suppose because chems have metal bodies, which
led to posts about chems and metal heads and black fluid and fiber-optic
wires. That was the only reason I bothered to shoot down the notion that she
and/or Agilus might be chems.

when it struck the stone wall of Agilus's cell. Outside the mine of the
man-apes, Severian had her down on the ground with his boot on her neck.
When she began to struggle again, he rapped her with the flat of his sword.
He doesn't actually say that he struck her head with it, but the blow
quieted her, so that seems most likely. Wherever he struck her with the
blade, he also failed to note any metal-on-metal sound.

Also, as I quoted, he struck her just below the ear with his hand, when they
were in Agilus's cell. If there was anything unusual about the head he
struck, he also failed to note that.

And I see no real connection between Agia and Hethor's lost sex doll.

-Roy

2010-06-19 Jerry Friedman replying to David Stockhoff #

Adds to the thread by noting that Agia is not especially Asian in appearance, citing the brown hair mentioned in The Shadow of the Torturer and the fair skin and freckles mentioned in The Sword of the Lictor. Responds to David Stockhoff's reply to Jeff Wilson, which argued that Agia and Typhon's Asian looks point to a deep-past origin.
supports · close-textual-reading

From: David Stockhoff <[email]>

They're not all that Asian in appearance. Agia has "brown hair" (/SotT/ Ch. XXI, referred to elsewhere too, I think) and "fair skin and freckles" (/SotL/ Ch. XXVIII, in a brief mention for comparison with Pia).

Jerry Friedman

>> After many chiliads of racial mixing from mechanized travel, there would be a  much smoother distribution of >>"Asian" features. Severian has seen many people with broad cheekbones and elongated eyes, at least by the >>time of his writing.
>
>Exactly---if A&A are particularly Asian in appearance, they could be from the deep past, before such mixing. If
>they are human, that is.

They're not all that Asian in appearance.  Agia has "brown hair" (/SotT/ Ch. XXI, referred to elsewhere too, I think) and "fair skin and freckles" (/SotL/ Ch. XXVIII, in a brief mention for comparison with Pia).

Jerry Friedman

2010-06-19 Gerry Quinn replying to Jerry Friedman #

Reports that the chems in the Long Sun books run on nuclear power, citing two episodes: Mint's azoth blasting a trooper's fusion power source, and Crane worrying about radioactive contamination after treating Lemur. Responds to Jerry Friedman's question about where the chems get their power, backing the claim with the scenes themselves.
answers · close-textual-reading · theories: Chems are nuclear powered

From: "Jerry Friedman" <[email]>

They are nuclear powered, although not necessarily all of the same type. Mint caused a trooper's fusion power source to blow when she struck him with an azoth. And Crane was concerned about [radioactive] isotope contamination after he treated Lemur similarly - he said it was good that he and Silk had been washed by their swim in Lake Limna.

- Gerry Quinn

2010-06-19 Gerry Quinn replying to Ryan Dunn #

Proposes that Agia and Agilus are both paracoitae, artificial beings from Hethor's box, so that the two could be twins of the same made kind, possibly running off together after a landing. Builds on Ryan Dunn's suggestion that Agia may be a paracoita, offering the idea that male and female paracoitae look alike and that Hethor may have recognised Agia as one of his kind.
proposes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is a paracoita; Agilus is a paracoita twin of Agia; Agia and Agilus ran off together after landing; Hethor recognised Agia as a paracoita

From: "Ryan Dunn" <[email]>

If Agia really was Hethor's paracoita rather than a human woman - I'm less than convinced of this, but there have been some interesting arguments made to that effect - might not Agilus have been another such? It might be that male and female paracoitae look similar, perhaps even having interchangeable parts. Male ones being slightly built would save weight and space after all, which would be convenient for someone who spends a lot of time in a box, or at least convenient for his or her owner. It would make them twins in a real sense. Perhaps they ran off together when their spacecraft landed.

Nobody has to change their face. In fact the possibility is added that Agia is a different paracoita other than the one lost by Hethor, but Hethor recognised her as being one, since they all have similar looks.

(If this is the case, I suspect the original owner of the rag shop met an unfortunate end.)

- Gerry Quinn

Whole message, with quotes · original

2010-06-19 Tom Hopkins replying to brunians #

Proposes that Merryn, not Agia, could be Hethor's missing paracoitae, citing Severian's descriptions of her as an ivory mask and a black-clad doll that reveal no life beneath. Grounds the idea in the Witches' Tower scene where Merryn and the Cumaean's true forms are revealed, and in the tone of Severian's descriptions.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Merryn is Hethor's missing paracoitae

Sorry
to intrude upon this interesting theory with what very well may be a pointless thought, however ignoring the predominate belief about Merryn being Severian's sister wouldn't she also be a viable case to be Hethors missing paracoitae?

When Severian meets Merryn and the Cumaean just previous to his meeting with Apu-Punchau, he describes his journey to the Witches Tower as a young apprentice and his meeting with the two exact same women now before him. In particular of Merryn he speaks:

"Here face was so lovely and so bloodless that it might have been a mask carved in ivory by some master sculptor."

Shortly after Merryn's and the Cumaean's true forms are revealed to him and the description of Merryn seems curious to me unless I am missing some rather obvious fact, which I admit could well be likely.

"Merryn had collapsed into a black-clad doll, so thin and dim that slender Dorcas seemed robust beside her. Now that intelligence no longer animated that ivory mask, I saw it was no more than parchment over bone"

I almost hesitate to mention this as I feel sure I am missing some important piece of information about why Merryn is perceived this way, however I feel it is worth mentioning none the less.

2010-06-19 Ryan Dunn replying to David Stockhoff #

Lists two passages from The Book of the New Sun that describe Agia's slanting or tilted eyes, offering them as the textual basis for reading her as of Asian descent. Replies to David Stockhoff, who had cited Severian's word 'slanting' in the thread on whether Agia is a robot.
supports · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is of Asian descent

Correct, David.

Here were the two passages that definitely indicated Agia was of some type of Asian descent (to me, anyhow)...

.

"With her upturned nose and strangely tilted eyes, she looked so much like her brother I felt sure they were twins." (TSotT, Chap. 17, "The Challenge")

"Beside me walked a suntanned girl with long and slanting eyes." (TCotA, Chap. 30, "The Corridors of Time")

.

...ryan

2010-06-20 Jeff Wilson replying to Gerry Quinn #

Proposes that Agia and Agilus are a bred line of made people, like the mastiff-men and cat women, engendered to produce clones that replace them. Answers Gerry Quinn's remark on Hethor's 'sun-kissed skin' by explaining that their look-alike incest and Hethor's attraction to Agia follow from shared creators' tastes.
proposes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia and Agilus are bred made people; Agia and Agilus were engendered to be replaced by clones; Hethor's doll resembles Agia because creators shared tastes

Perhaps Agilus and Agia are the remnants of a race or a bloodline of sexdoll-people, engender for that purpose and of similar origin as the mastiff-men and the cat women. They look alike because they are not just twins but made to be fertile only among their cohort and giving birth to clones of themselves meant to eventually replace them. This would explain why their incest is "acceptable" and thus not cursed with defects from hidden recessives (because they don't come with recessives) but the sin of their creation is visited upon them.

This also explains why Hethor falls for Agia; she greatly resembles his doll because their creators had similar opinions on what made a most desirable woman, long term. (Dr Talos has more radical preferences for Jolenta, no doubt contributing to her unsustainability.)

2010-06-20 Jerry Friedman replying to Jeff Wilson #

Declines to answer whether Agia is a robot by pointing to Thecla's narrow hands and khaibit instead, and rejects Jeff Wilson's sharp-sound comparison. Responds to Wilson's arguments that Agia's aureole could be dust or gold skin tone and that a sexbot would not cast curses, noting the modesty after nakedness.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is a robot

From: "[email]" <[email]>

I won't answer that because I'm afraid to get into a game with ancient, inviolable rules that I don't know. But if I were to answer it, I'd say Thecla and presumably her khaibit.

Here I'd mention Thecla's khaibit. I'd also say that Thecla has "impossibly narrow" hands, which I picture as long and thin, like her body. You may or may not picture them that way, and however you picture them may or may not be what you mean by "small or delicate".

From: Jeff Wilson <[email]>

...

That's worse than reading about Allowin's Necklace.

However, "sharp" is not the word I'd use to distinguish those two sounds. Maybe just me.

I live in a fairly rural area, I suppose I could round up a cattle skull, put a wig on it, and record it smacking it into stone surface.

This is modesty *after* she has been seen completely naked. And why would anyone program a sexbot to cast runic curses?

I do not see that Agia is an assassin, her murders do not appear to serve anyone's purposes outside hers and her brothers.

Rereading about their "aureate faces" and "a narrow beam of sunlight that had given his head, and Agia's, an aureole a few moments before", it could be the dust and fibers from the straw on the floor, the way mist makes an aureole around the moon's sunlit face.

http://www.lpod.org/?p=702

or since "aureate" can also eman decorated with gold, there might be no actual fringe of light, but instead this would be another reference to their skin tone, which Sev has already likened to gold where the sun glints from Agia's skin through a rent in her ragged gown.

I'm not saying this doesn't count as a subtextual halo, of course.

That's not the same as a sexbot - "sleeping" immobile in a box is a strong advertisement that you are not really a person.

-- Jeff Wilson - [email]

< http://ieeetamut.org >

> http://www.youtube.com/watch?v=auss-QFCor4&feature=PlayList&p=4C9AAD000C45EE30&
> playnext_from=PL&playnext=1&index=18
>
> http://preview.tinyurl.com/2dy5gd2

That's worse than reading about Allowin's Necklace.

However, "sharp" is not the word I'd use to distinguish those two sounds.  Maybe just me.

I live in a fairly rural area, I suppose I could round up a cattle skull, put a wig on it, and record it smacking it into stone surface.

> Second, she may have something to hide, vs having modesty, although
> modesty could just as well be "programmed." For all I know, she has a
> penis.

This is modesty *after* she has been seen completely naked. And why would anyone program a sexbot to cast runic curses?

> Third, you are exactly right about Agia's character. Severian is a
> torturer; I propose that Agia is an assassin; Brunians proposes that she
> is a whore. His theory works better in some ways than mine.

I do not see that Agia is an assassin, her murders do not appear to serve anyone's purposes outside hers and her brothers.

> Fourth, the aureole is not of hair, but that's an interesting
> observation. More importantly, it covers the pair, not Agia. The two
> together are more than either of them individually.

Rereading about their "aureate faces" and "a narrow beam of sunlight that had given his head, and Agia's, an aureole a few moments before", it could be the dust and fibers from the straw on the floor, the way mist makes an aureole around the moon's sunlit face.

http://www.lpod.org/?p=702

or since "aureate" can also eman decorated with gold, there might be no actual fringe of light, but instead this would be another reference to their skin tone, which Sev has already likened to gold where the sun glints from Agia's skin through a rent in her ragged gown.

I'm not saying this doesn't count as a subtextual halo, of course.

> And if she's supposed to be a fully functional android, it would
> hardly do for her to go around boasting of her strength and her
> nuclear power cell.

That's not the same as a sexbot - "sleeping" immobile in a box is a strong advertisement that you are not really a person.

2010-06-20 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Agia is not a hard robot, pointing to the poppet's box stretches and the sharp sound episode to show her muscles relax when her consciousness is disrupted. Supports this by citing her ruthlessness, possible innate strength from the android line's domestic design, and her willingness to submit to Hethor's tastes for revenge.
proposes · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is not a hard robot

I think the poppet's stretches in the box are not suited to anything like a vertebrate: the bit about smiling when it closes so she can be smiling when it opens indicates her muscles don't relax during periods of inactivity. The whole sharp sound episode demonstrates Agila's muscles positively relax when her consciousness is interfered with.

As for her strength, she's ruthless and cold, like Lady MacBeth sought to be. She may also have a bit more innate strength than usual for her size, perhaps because rather than just sex toys, A&A's line was intended to be conjugal domestics; this way a slight manservant can be just as good at opening jars and moving furniture as a full-size. And it may not be muscle, it could be a more ready adrenaline rush.

Agia owns up to doing all the kinky things that Hethor likes, so she's at least capable of bottoming if not actual submission. I think her determination is such that she could and would submit to anything short of a SAW movie that would further her revenge on Severian and the world in general, at least in the short term.

2010-06-20 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that Agia need not be a robot, weighing a bio-android or genetically engineered human explanation against the robot theory, and asks what role the poppet idea plays. Responds to Gerry Quinn's proposal of an engineered sexdoll race by saying it is easier to posit an unknown race than to justify a robot assassin, and it rests its case on the poppet and live-puppet themes.
disputes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is a bio-android or genetically engineered human; Agia is the poppet; Agia is a hard robot

That's an interesting suggestion. It's a way to retain the "sacred twins" theory without the robot angle. Funny how it's easier to propose an entire unknown race (perhaps since we know such must exist) than to explain why someone would create an assassin robot or why a sexbot would turn holy and self-directed.

The robot theory is based entirely on Agia's (1) possibly metal head and (2) possible identity as the poppet. The bio-android theory doesn't need #1. As Roy notes, there are no other hints that she is metal despite many opportunities, and if we accept as simple truth their twin origin, Agilus' death disproves the metalhead clue for me. But Agia remains an extreme femme fatale---emphasis on both words. Why?

The Asian-appearance thing is irrelevant to this except to the extent it suggests a non-Nessus origin for her and "explains" the use of ninja and assassin weapons.

I'd prefer to rest a theory on at least 3 data points. If she's not a "hard robot":

---Her strength has another source
---She could still be the poppet
---Agilus could be her actual twin IF she's a genetic/flesh android or just human

After giving away the hard-robot part, is it needed that A&A be anything other than human at all? It's not strictly necessary that Agia be the poppet; it may be enough that Hethor recognizes something in Agia that he likes---it could be as simple as that she is passive in bed and dominating/controlling otherwise, although I doubt her ability to even fake being passive if she's human. Perhaps Hethor needs both dominance and submission.

Still, I believe the poppet story must lead somewhere. We know Wolfe really likes the live-puppet theme, as a subcategory of the live-robot theme. It's actually hard to imagine a long novel by Wolfe without it. It has to be in there somewhere. Jonas is one twist on that theme.

2010-06-20 Jerry Friedman replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues against the robot reading of Agia and considers that Thecla's anger, Agilus's death and Severian's survival explain her hostility to Severian. Replies to António Pedro Marques, citing the Ch. XIX remark about the hopeful, hopeless courage of the poor and the shop as her cover for assassination.
disputes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Agia is not a robot; Agia is an assassin using the costume shop as cover

From: Ant?nio Pedro Marques <[email]>

Second most important? :-)

As people have pointed out, she's good at getting people to do what she wants, and she controls Hethor, who controls the monsters.

I think part of the point may be that for Severian to play his role, he needs supernatural powers and help, but the antagonist to all that is most fully human, unscrupulousness and hatred will go a long way. Plus some mirrors.

Well, Thecla appears in another way.

I don't think law or morality has anything to do with her feelings, and I think her anger has something to do with Severian's failure to die (a point Agilus brought up). It may also have to do with his ability to resist her efforts to change his mind. And after Agilus dies, what does she have but revenge?

Their instant scheme is very striking. Maybe we should imagine that, with lots of time on their hands since their shop isn't busy, they've talked about what to do if someone brings something valuable in.

This is a stereotype of crime fiction, I think--the grasping, ruthless character ready with a deception at a moment's notice. It's more understandable among those who possess "the hopeful, hopeless courage of the poor" (/SotT/, Ch. XIX).

As I said, I don't agree.

Incidentally, if she is a sex robot or a genetically engineered doxy or some such, it's remarkable that she'd be eking out a living from the costume shop. I suppose if she's an assassin, the shop could be her cover.

Okay, we agree on that.

Jerry Friedman

2010-06-20 David Stockhoff replying to Jerry Friedman #

Argues that Agia can be understood as an ordinary human rather than a robot or otherworldly being, while still being important. Responds to Jerry Friedman by listing Agia's light imagery, her effect on Severian, her name, and her link to Agilus as features needing explanation, and says her role is chiefly an antagonist to Severian.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is not a robot

Agia becomes simpler if you drop any notion of being synthetic or otherworldly. But not less important.

She still has (1) light-based descriptions (2) the effect she has on Severian (spiritual burning) (3) her name (4) her relationship with Agilus and so on. These need to be accounted for.

The fact is that Agia's whole purpose from beginning to end seems to be to work as an antagonist to Severian.

As I said, I don't agree.

Jerry Friedman

2010-06-20 Jeff Wilson replying to Jerry Friedman #

Speculates that Agia and Agilus, the two travellers in Avern duels, were dismissed from service and turned to mending and shop-minding, and that Agia's magical inscription ties them to revolutionary magicians opposing the New Sun. Grounds this in Agilus's failed execution by avern, Severian's belief in a scheme, and Agilus's silence when accused in his cell.
proposes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia and Agilus are anti-New Sun conspirators; Agia's inscription ties her to Vodalus

It must seem so unfair that they did everything *right* and they still end up getting caught because the mark was superhumanly immune to the invariably fatal avern.

Severian believes it is an ongoing scheme, and Agilus does not deny it when accused of it in his cell.

They both have beyond poor attitudes, so I could see A&A being dismissed from whatever service they might have had and forced to get by on their domestic skils: mending clothes and minding a shop, and titilating sugar daddies or mammas as the opportunity presents.

Though, there is a possibility that A&A are both: her hasty magical inscription ties her to the unknown number of faux magicians who oppose the the advent of the New Sun, and through them to Vodalus' revolutionaries. This anti-Illuminati conspiracy may have encompassed them in their searched for a sacred hermaphrodite, and kept them on as deniable assets, you never know when a ruthless killer will come in handy.

Argues that Agia may be a poppet, though that would require time travel since Boatman, Ouen and others vouch for her. Responds to brunians by noting that the scene jumps from Hethor's rant to Dorcas with a daisy, which is inconsistent since Hethor traveled with Dorcas and Severian.
informs · close-textual-reading · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: Agia is a poppet; Poppet explanation requires time travel

.

2010-06-21 John Watkins replying to David Stockhoff #

Speculates that Agia may be a robot or doll-like figure, proposing that the paracoita motif relates to puppets and marionettes across the Book of the New Sun. Grounds this in Severian's marionette dreams, the White Fountain's phrase "marionette of flesh," the lemonwood detail, and possible puns on Wolfe's Catholic faith, while suggesting the puzzle may be a motif rather than a single solution.
proposes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is a robot or doll; paracoita is a motif not a single character; marionette imagery links Severian and Baldanders

If only Gene Wolfe had written another text, possibly a short story, exploring the idea of puppets in boxes. Then maybe someone wiser than I could draw wisdom from that text and apply it to the problem of the paracoita, which is beyond me.

I will make a few observations that, while not wise, may spur others to wisdom.

1) It seems that delicate and/or doll-like characteristics are attributed to several women in the story, but that Hethor meant one particular paracoita--we may just be dealing with a motif, rather than a puzzle with a definite character as the solution, and there may be red herrings...like a sickening crack of bone on stone being described as as sharp as a metallic clang. Or not.

2) Severian dreams of marionettes that seemingly represent him and Baldanders.

3) In Urth, Severian-as-White-Fountain describes Severian-as-Man as a "marionette of flesh".

4) An inveterate punster who was also a devout Catholic might be taken by the term "marionette." It might conceivably please him to make several female characters in a single work "marionettes" in one sense or another.

5) Why lemonwood? After all, the classic wood for a doll that comes to life is pine. Per Google, the Howdy-Doody marionette was lemonwood.

Whole message, with quotes · original
If only Gene Wolfe had written another text, possibly a short story,
exploring the idea of puppets in boxes.  Then maybe someone wiser than I
could draw wisdom from that text and apply it to the problem of the
paracoita, which is beyond me.

I will make a few observations that, while not wise, may spur others to
wisdom.

1)  It seems that delicate and/or doll-like characteristics are attributed
to several women in the story, but that Hethor meant one particular
paracoita--we may just be dealing with a motif, rather than a puzzle with a
definite character as the solution, and there may be red herrings...like a
sickening crack of bone on stone being described as as sharp as a metallic
clang.  Or not.

2)  Severian dreams of marionettes that seemingly represent him and
Baldanders.

3)  In Urth, Severian-as-White-Fountain describes Severian-as-Man as a
"marionette of flesh".

4)  An inveterate punster who was also a devout Catholic might be taken by
the term "marionette."  It might conceivably please him to make several
female characters in a single work "marionettes" in one sense or another.

5)  Why lemonwood?  After all, the classic wood for a doll that comes to
life is pine.  Per Google, the Howdy-Doody marionette was lemonwood.

2010-06-21 Ryan Dunn replying to John Watkins #

Disputes John Watkins's claim that the Howdy-Doody marionette image fits Agia as a sex toy, saying the marionette is at most a motif and the imagery is wrong. Backs the point with dictionary definitions of poppet, paracoita, genicon and scopolagna, terms for a small beloved, a female partner, an imagined partner and a stimulating woman.
disputes · theories: Agia as a marionette sex toy

Not sure if I'm buying the sex toy as a marionette. A motif, maybe, but the wrong imagery from what I have read. Maybe I'm misunderstanding you.

.

poppet
n. - small human figure used in witchcraft and sorcery c.1300 n. - poetically, a loved one, particularly when the loved one is physically smaller than the lover

paracoita
n. - a female sexual partner

genicon
n. - a sexual partner imagined by one who is dissatisfied by his/her actual partner

scopolagna
n. - a woman whose appearance others find stimulating in the extreme

.

...ryan

2010-06-21 John Watkins replying to Ryan Dunn #

Clarifies that the earlier remark linked Agia, a doll, to lemonwood through Howdy Doody's wooden construction and the lemonwood box, rather than claiming Howdy Doody was literally made of lemonwood. Responds to Ryan Dunn's query about whether Howdy Doody or only his box was lemonwood, explaining that a doll stored in a box of that wood would be the alternative route to the association.
answers · close-textual-reading · theories: Agia is associated with lemonwood through her storage box

I was noting that one reason to associate a doll with lemonwood was that Howdy Doody was made of lemonwood. Obviously the "doll" in question here would not literally be made of wood, so if one wanted to associate wood with such a doll one would have to use an alternative route, e.g., by having that doll stored in a box of that wood.

Whole message, with quotes · original

would not literally be made of wood, so if one wanted to associate wood with
such a doll one would have to use an alternative route, e.g., by having that
doll stored in a box of that wood.

2010-06-21 David Duffy replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Agia and Agilus could be genetically similar twins, citing the genetic engineering of khaibits in Urth as making such twinning plausible rather than mere sibling resemblance. Responds to David Stockhoff's suggestion that Agia could be Agilus's actual twin, and adds a light remark that Agia becomes Severian's evil twin after Agilus is lost.
supports · theology-and-religion · source-and-allusion · theories: Agia and Agilus are genetically identical twins

Well, it's a common trope that opposite-sex dizygotic twins can be so similar that they can cross-dress and confuse others, but in reality they are will have an average brother-sister resemblance. But in Urth, we know about the khaibits, so it would be possible to produce genetically identical twins aside from the sex chromosomes. That would be closer to the alchemical twinning, I guess. I always had the impression they were unusually similar, but couldn't point to any particular descriptive passages.

I guess the other thing is after losing Agilus, she becomes Severian's evil twin ;)

Cheers, David Duffy.

2010-06-21 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that the word "poppet" is simply a variant of "puppet", and suggests the paracoita could also be a poppet in the sense of a witchcraft figure, since Hethor is a sorcerer of sorts. Responds to John Watkins's objection to reading the sex toy as a marionette, drawing on the dictionary definitions he quoted for poppet, paracoita and genicon.
proposes · source-and-allusion · theories: Paracoita may be a poppet in the witchcraft sense; Agia's sex toy is a marionette

But poppet obviously = puppet.

However, the paracoita may have been a poppet in your first sense too, since Hethor is a sorcerer of sorts.

2010-06-21 Eugene Zaretskiy replying to David Stockhoff #

Answers David Stockhoff's question about which collection a story appears in, saying it is in the recent The Best of Gene Wolfe. Praises that collection for including Wolfe's own commentary on each story, which the poster finds funny in places and insightful elsewhere.
answers

I read it in the recent "The Best of Gene Wolfe" collection.

http://www.amazon.com/Best-Gene-Wolfe-Definitive-Retrospective/dp/076532136X/ref=sr_1_5?ie=UTF8&s=books&qid=1277147539&sr=8-5

I particularly like this collection because every story has commentary by Gene Wolfe and quite a few of them are hilarious, and the rest simply insightful.
